===PAGE 4=== (ocr, 18 lines)
   1|                                                                         In Memoriam
   2|                                 James J. Larkin, affectionately known as Jim, left the world on March 31, 2024, leav-
   3|                                 ing a legacy of love and laughter. Born on June 24, 1940, in Newton, MA, Jim's reli-
   4|                                    able good nature and funny wit made him a beacon of joy to all who met him. Jim
   5|                                    was a devoted husband to his wife Sheila of 45 years, who survives him. He was a
   6|                                   very positive role model and loving stepfather to Darrell Maki. He was preceded in
   7|                                  death by his parents, Robert and Mary (Lynch) Larkin and his brother Robert Larkin
   8|                                                                                                Jr.
   9|                                  Jim was a dedicated systems engineer in the technology sector at GenRad and Poly-
  10|                                    com before his retirement. He was a passionate member of the Maserati Club, the
  11|                                     Mid State Antique Auto Club, and the Antique Auto Club of America. May God
  12|                                                 grant him a front row seat at the Antique Auto shows in Heaven.
  13|                                   His interest in family genealogy and the history of Lunenburg led him to become a
  14|                                  curator for the Lunenburg Historical Society, a role he cherished, especially Whalom
  15|                                  Park memorabilia. He served in the US Navy from 1961-1966. Jim was also an avid
  16|                                  metal detector, always looking for hidden treasures. Jim, his siblings, and family fre-
  17|                                                    quently shared many stories of their bird watching sightings.
  18|                                                                                                4

===PAGE 5=== (ocr, 12 lines)
   1|                                                                          In Memoriam
   2|                                              Wayne 'Fuzzy' Balcom was born in Fitchburg February 8th. 1936.
   3|                                  He went to Lunenburg schools and graduated in 1955. He then went into the Army.
   4|                            Fuzzy was appointed to the Fire Department August 9th 1953 and retired February5th. 2001
   5|                               after 47 years. Fuzzy was on the original "house men" in the station in the early 1050's.
   6|                                                         He then served as driver on Group 2 for many years.
   7|                                                      In his retirement, he was a fixture at the Tuesday morning
   8|                                                retirees meeting and became the department unofficial historian.
   9|                                                  He was Sealer of Weights and Measures from 1969 until 1978.
  10|                                             He worked at Simonds Saw & Steel for 43 years. He retired in 2001.
  11|                                                       Fuzzy passed away September 24th. 2024. He was 88.
  12|                                                                                                5

===PAGE 6=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                     LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                    YEAR INCORPORATED-1728
   3|                                                                  REGISTERED VOTERS-9,468
   4|                                                                        FY '24 TAX RATE- $14.10
   5|                                      OPEN TOWN GOVERNMENT- 5 MEMBER SELECT BOARD
   6|                                           ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                            ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                     TOTAL AREA- 26.63 MILES
   9|                Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
  10|                  quare miles. Lunenburg is locatea in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles from Bo in, It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by th
  11|               Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by
  12|                the Town of Townsend.
  13|                Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting. Subject to the legislative decisions
  14|                made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered by an elected five member
  15|                Select Board and a Town Manager and local school affairs are administered by an elected seven
  16|               member School Committee.
  17|                Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Lake Whalom and the for-
  18|                mer Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This
  19|                district was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of
  20|                Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect charac-
  21|               teristics of these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills Lake area was developed as
  22|                a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. Shirley Reservoir is a public water body also
  23|                developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round residences.
  24|                 Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two
  25|                major arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of
  26|                Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards have
  27|                slowly developed into single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg, In 2013 the Annual
  28|                Town Meeting voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance
  29|                farming in the community and to administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw,
  30|                 The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District")
  31|                 bylaw to regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" struc-
  32|                 tures as defined by the bylaw within an established bounded district. The bylaw was introduced at
  33|                 Town Meeting along with the Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to en-
  34|                 courage mixed-use commercial and residential development. While the Village District Bylaw en-
  35|                 courages the economic vitalization of downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District
  36|                 Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the expense of the town's historical character and integrity.
  37|              Scan the QR Code to visit our website at https://www.lunenburgma.gov
  38|              Follow us on Facebook at https://www.facebook.com/townoflunenburgma
  39|              Follow us on Instagram at https://instagram.com/townoflunenburgma
  40|                                                                                                6

===PAGE 17=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                           REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                                                                   Fiscal Year 2024
   3|              Note: The Fiscal Year 2024 Report of the Town Manager was not authored by Town Manager Heather Lemieux. The content
   4|              herein is based on a review and synthesis of periodic Town Manager Reports provided to the Select Board.
   5|              Fiscal Year 2024 (FY24) was a year of meaningful progress for the Town of Lunenburg, marked by the advancement of long-
   6|              planned capital and infrastructure projects, significant strides in restoring the public's confidence in the property tax assessment
   7|              process, important legislative wins for our senior residents, a coordinated response to multiple storm events, and continued work
   8|             to address staffing needs across Town departments. Despite ongoing challenges, the dedicated staff, appointed and elected offi-
   9|              cials, and volunteers of the community continued to deliver a high level of service to Lunenburg residents. Notably, Town Man-
  10|              ager Heather Lemieux tendered her resignation, after over seven years of service, to the Select Board on June 4, 2024.
  11|              Below are the highlights of the key topics and accomplishments from Fiscal Year 2024 within the Office of the Town Manager.
  12|              Town Manager Goals
  13|              As in prior years, the Town Manager's goals for FY2024 centered on three primary areas: continuing to advance the redevelop-
  14|              ment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, review of financial policies and address long-term staffing and unknown infrastructure
  15|              needs to help inform the budget process, and employee recruitment, recognition, and retention. Progress was made across all three
  16|              areas during the fiscal year.
  17|              925 Massachusetts Avenue Redevelopment
  18|              The redevelopment of 925 Massachusetts Avenue remained a top Town Manager goal throughout FY2024, with substantial pro-
  19|              gress made toward readying the site for disposal and future reuse.
  20|             In the fall of 2023, the EPA-funded contractor completed the removal of the hydraulic lift and underground holding tank at the
  21|              site, with hazardous materials submitted for proper off-site disposal. The Town's environmental consultant, Joe McLaughlin of the
  22|              BETA Group, continued to coordinate with the EPA and MassDEP on follow-up environmental work. The EPA approved addi-
  23|              tional Brownfields funding to conduct soil borings, monitoring well installations, and groundwater sampling to address remaining
  24|              data gaps beneath the building.
  25|              In February 2024, the Town Manager met with MassDevelopment and their consultant from BSC Group, engaged through the
  26|              Real Estate Technical Assistance program, to develop conceptual plans, perform a market analysis, and draft a Request for Pro-
  27|              posals for the disposal of the property. A site visit took place in March 2024, with work on a strict timeline to expend the technical
  28|              assistance funds by June 30, 2024. The Historical Society also expressed interest in preserving the cupola atop the 925 Mass Ave
  29|              building, and the Town Manager referred the request to the APDC for review and incorporation into the property's disposition
  30|              plans. Conceptual plans yielded two options: maintaining the same footprint of the building if it were to be a renovation and one
  31|              that would be new construction.
  32|              Infrastructure, Facilities, and Capital Projects
  33|              Flat Hill Culvert Project
  34|              The Flat Hill Road Culvert replacement was a major project in FY2024. Funded in part by a $500,000 Small Bridge Grant, the
  35|               roject went out to bid in early 2024 with the lowest responsive and responsible bid coming in at $830,230. The Town Manager
  36|               uccessfully secured an additional $100,000 from MassDOT to cover higher-than-estimated costs, bringing total available funding
  37|              to approximately $975,000. Construction was planned to begin in the fall of 2024 when lake water levels are lowered, with an
  38|              estimated two-month road closure.
  39|              Page Street Culvert Project
  40|              The Page Street Culvert replacement, performed by Powell Stone & Gravel, was completed in September 2023 on time and under
  41|              budget. The road was closed briefly to allow excavation and installation of the new pipe, and the guardrail was installed shortly
  42|              thereafter.
  43|                                                                                               17

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|               LED Streetlight Conversion l
   2|               The LED streetlight conversion project, awarded to Pine Ridge Technologies, was completed in December 2023. Approximately
   3|               358 streetlights were converted throughout Town. The project was funded through the Green Communities Grant, and upon
   4|               completion, the Town received a finalized Unitil incentive of $37,900, exceeding the originally projected $33,400. Total grant-
   5|                eligible expenditures were approximately $139,000. The Town also finalized an Outdoor Light Billing Agreement with Unitil
   6|               Senior Center ADA Patio
   7|               The Senior Center ADA Patio project, long in capital planning, was completed and celebrated with a ribbon cutting ceremony on
   8|               October 11, 2023. The project was the result of collaborative effort over many years and represents a significant improvement in
   9|               accessibility and outdoor programming space at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center.
  10|               TC Passios Building
  11|               Persistent roof leaks at the TC Passios building required ongoing attention throughout FY2024. The Facilities Department
  12|               patched multiple leak areas and sought quotes for a phased roof repair, with a warrant article submitted to the Special Town
  13|               Meeting. In April 2024, a significant leak damaged the Public Access studio. The Town Manager and Facilities Director decided
  14|               to relocate the studio to the dry rear wing of the building, which occurred after the Annual Town Meeting.
  15|               Public Safety Building
  16|               In December 2023, a severe rain and wind event tore off a 30-foot section of metal siding at the Public
  17|               Safety Building, exposing the fire apparatus bays. The Facilities Director responded immediately, securing
  18|               the building that same day. Insurance covered the siding repair, which was completed in the spring of
  19|               2024. The Public Safety Carpet replacement project was also bid in late 2023, with bids due in December.
  20|               The bid was awarded to Atkinson Carpet and Flooring in the amount of $43,790.
  21|               Welcome to Lunenburg Sign
  22|               A "Welcome to Lunenburg" sign that was provided to the town by the North Central Chamber of Commerce and was installed
  23|               on Lancaster Avenue, near the Stillman Farm.
  24|               Lunenburg Adult Activity Center Generator
  25|               The LAAC Generator project, included in the FY23 Capital Plan, was bid in the fall of 2023. Four bids were received and the
  26|               project was awarded to Morse Brothers of Westminster for $99,940. With a generator lead time of 16-18 weeks, installation was
  27|               completed in the spring of 2024.
  28|               Marshall Park
  29|               The Marshall Park revitalization project advanced significantly in FY2024. A Request for Qualifications for an Owner's Project
  30|               Manager was issued in early 2024. After the Park Commission evaluated respondents and conducted interviews, the Town final-
  31|               ized a contract with the selected OPM for a not-to-exceed fee of $250,000, covering services through designer/engineer selection
  32|               and completion of PARC Grant-funded construction by June 2025. The Parks Commission also engaged a firm for the prelimi-
  33|               nary permitting required for the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers permit related to the $1,000,000 Land and Water Conservation
  34|               Fund grant. The Town also finalized the Right of Entry Agreement with the Lunenburg Skate Park organization, with construc-
  35|               tion planned to begin in 2024.
  36|               Roadway Maintenance and Infrastructure
  37|               Roadway maintenance was active throughout FY2024. Sealcoating was completed in the fall of 2023 on twelve roads. PJ Keat-
  38|               ing paved Elmwood Road and West Groton Road in the spring of 2024. Premier Fence completed guardrail repairs on North-
  39|               field Road, Leominster Shirley Road, and Graham Street. The DPW crew continued routine maintenance including pothole re-
  40|               pairs, drainage clearing, roadside vegetation cutting, and sign repairs throughout the year. The Town also received a $21,979
  41|               Rural Roads apportionment from a MassDOT supplemental transportation bond distribution in April 2024.
  42|               Route 2A Road Safety Audit
  43|               A Road Safety Audit was conducted in September 2023 by VHB and MassDOT, with the Town Manager, Police Chief, Fire
  44|                Chief, and DPW Director participating. MassDOT repainted lane markings on Route 2A in October 2023, and the final RSA
  45|               report was submitted in November 2023. MassDOT subsequently developed a work order to address additional low-cost safety
  46|               improvements at the two intersections.
  47|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|               Financial Management
   2|               Tax Assessment and Billing
   3|               FY2024 continued to involve significant complexity in tax assessment and billing. With the Town's property revaluation not
   4|               completed until February 2024, the Bureau of Local Assessment approved a valuation delay, and the Town issued third quarter
   5|               preliminary tax bills using a Pro Forma Recap approved by DOR in December 2023. The Treasurer/Collector and Interim Princi-
   6|               pal Assessor, Rena Swezey, worked extensively to test billing processes in Munis before generating final bills, and a detailed tax
   7|               bill insert was prepared to explain the unusual billing cycle to taxpayers.
   8|               After the FY24 fourth quarter actual tax bills were mailed, the Assessors Department discovered a software error in Munis that
   9|               caused an incorrect total taxable value to be printed on bills for property owners with exemptions. The payable amount on every
  10|               bill was correct. The Town proactively sent correction letters to all affected taxpayers with copies of the accurate Vision Property
  11|               Cards, and a ticket was submitted to Tyler Technologies to resolve the issue.
  12|               FY25 preliminary tax billing also required outside contractor assistance due to persistent software challenges with Tyler Tech-
  13|               nologies. These bills incorporated the estimated additional tax levy from the override approved by voters in May 2024.
  14|               The Town's New Growth for FY24 came in at approximately $630,000, exceeding the original estimate by $230,000 and provid-
  15|               ing a meaningful addition to the tax base.
  16|               Community Compact Best Practices Grants
  17|               In October 2023, both Community Compact Best Practices grant requests were approved. The Town received $25,000 for devel-
  18|                  ment of a bridge and culvert inventory and asset management plan. The tinancial management policies and procedures upda
  19|                  as administered directly by the Division of Local Services Financial Management Resource Bureau. with the goal of having
  20|               complete draft reviewed and presented to the Select Board for adoption by spring 2024.
  21|               FY2025 Budget
  22|               The Town Manager presented the Five-Year Financial Forecast in January 2024 and the FY2025 Preliminary Budget in Febru-
  23|               ary. The budget process included Finance Committee hearings from late February through late March and a public hearing on
  24|               March 28th, with the Annual Town Meeting on May 4, 2024. Health insurance rate increases for active plans came in at 5.7%, significantly below the initial 10% estimate. Chapter 90 apportionment for FY2025 was $418,227. The Town also signed a new
  25|               electricity supply contract with BP at 11.77 cents per kWh for 48 months, effective November 2024.
  26|               Emergency Response and Storm Events
  27|               FY2024 included several notable weather emergencies. On September 11, 2023, approximately seven inches of rain fell over-
  28|                 ight, causing significant flooding locally and catastrophic damage in the neighboring City of Leominster. Lunenburg's Fire De
  29|                 artment deployed four apparatus to assist with evacuations in Leominster, while the DPW crew worked through midnight clear
  30|               ing roads and drains. The DPW Director submitted an estimated $24,000 in storm-related costs for potential state relief funding.
  31|               Emergency Response and Storm Events
  32|               FY2024 included several notable weather emergencies. On September 11, 2023, approximately seven inches of rain fell over-
  33|               night, causing significant flooding locally and catastrophic damage in the neighboring City of Leominster. Lunenburg's Fire De-
  34|               partment deployed four apparatus to assist with evacuations in Leominster, while the DPW crew worked through midnight clear-
  35|               ing roads and drains. The DPW Director submitted an estimated $24,000 in storm-related costs for potential state relief funding.
  36|               In November 2023, the Town's first snow and ice event of the season saw DPW crews responding overnight, with Highway Su-
  37|               perintendent Joe Progin going out on Thanksgiving to address uncovered manholes exposed during plowing. In December 2023,
  38|               a significant wind and rain storm damaged the Public Safety Building siding and caused widespread flooding and downed trees,
  39|               requiring a coordinated response from Facilities, DPW, Police, and Fire.
  40|               Senior Means-Tested Property Tax Exemption
  41|               A significant legislative achievement of FY2024 was the passage of Home Rule Petition House Bill 3911, authorizing the Town
  42|               to establish a Senior Citizen Means-Tested Property Tax Exemption. The Town Manager had worked with Representative Scars-
  43|               dale on this effort since early in the fiscal year, submitting written testimony to the Joint Committee on Revenue in July 2023.
  44|               The petition was favorably reported out of committee in November 2023, advanced through the House and Senate, and reached
  45|               the Governor's desk in April 2024.
  46|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|               Information Technology
   2|               The new Town website, built through CivicPlus, launched in October 2023. The MUNIS accounting system upgrade went live in
   3|               the fall of 2023, though ongoing Tyler Technologies software issues required outside contractor assistance for multiple billing
   4|               processes. When the IT Network Administrator position became vacant, the Town increased its Suzor IT service agreement to
   5|               provide an on-site technician five days per week. Network penetration testing was also completed, funded through a MIIA Flex Grant using the Town's insurance rewards credits.
   6|               Staffing and Personnel
   7|               Staffing continued to be a major focus in FY2024. Notably, the Finance Director, Assistant Town Accountant, Conservation Ad-
   8|               ministrator, Land Use Director, and Principal Assessor positions were all actıvely managed through vacancies, interim arrange-
   9|                ments, and recruitment. Notably, Land Use Director Adam Burney retired in October 2023 after nine years of service, Interim
  10|               Planner Richard Harris provided continuity, and Building Commissioner Brian Gingras stepped into the Land Use managerial
  11|               role. Executive Assistant Elaine Peterson retired in March 2024, with Annie Aubrey transitioning into the role. A new Planning
  12|               Board Administrative Assistant, Logan O'Connell, joined the team in the spring of 2024.
  13|               Grants and External Funding
  14|               The Town was active in pursuing grants and external funding throughout FY2024. Highlights include:
  15|                    $206,000 Department of Mental Health grant for a Co-Response Mental Health Clinician (Police Department).
  16|                    $37,900 Unitil incentive for LED streetlight conversion project.
  17|                    $25,000 Community Compact Best Practices Grant for bridge and culvert asset management.
  18|                    $61,305 MassTrails Grant for the Rayno-Table Rock Cowdrey Parking Planning project.
  19|                    $240,503 Fair Share Amendment infrastructure distribution.
  20|                    $100,000 additional MassDOT funding for the Flat Hill Culvert project.
  21|                    $30,000 State storm relief allocation from the September 2023 flooding.
  22|                    $21,979 Rural Roads supplemental apportionment from MassDOT.
  23|                    $18,800 Firefighter Safety Grant for power saws and portable radios.
  24|                    $3,500 Emergency Management Performance Grant for cold water rescue equipment.
  25|                    $33,910 MILO Advanced Training Simulation System Police Department earmark included in the FY25 House budget.
  26|               Long-Term Planning
  27|               The Master Plan Steering Committee launched a community survey in late 2023 and into 2024 as part of updating the Town's
  28|               comprehensive plan.
  29|               Annual Town Meeting and Election
  30|               The Annual Town Meeting was held on Saturday, May 4, 2024 at the MS/HS Auditorium. The Annual Town Election was held
  31|               on Saturday, May 18, 2024. In addition to electing members to several boards, voters approved an override question, which pro-
  32|               vided additional tax levy capacity.
  33|               Respectfully submitted,
  34|               Jennifer Warren-Dyment,
  35|               on behalf of
  36|               Heather R. Lemieux
  37|               Town Manager,
  38|               Town of Lunenburg
  39|               Fiscal Year 2024

===PAGE 31=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                                                                        BOARD OF ASSESSORS
   2|             Under the umbrella of Massachusetts state and local town laws, Lunenburg's Assessing organization will work with the town opera-
   3|             tions and our citizens to meet our vision for delivering a fair and equitable taxation for our citizens. The Lunenburg Board of Asses-
   4|             sors will evolve and administer a series of "Best Practices" methodologies consistent our Vision. These practices will ensure equita-
   5|             ble treatment and compassion for all our citizens.
   6|             The Board of Assessors has empowered an Assessor organization that has the capability to evaluate and assess all property at its full
   7|             and fair market value. This is accomplished through a rigorous measurement of property, collection appropriate data, analysis and
   8|             maintenance of all property tax data records. Additionally, the Assessor department administers a motor vehicle excise tax, real es-
   9|             tate and motor vehicle tax abatements, and all statutory tax exemptions as allowed by law. The Assessor Organization will work co-
  10|             hesively with other town departments.
  11|             The last year has seen a lot of progress in restoring the fully functional Assessors' office.
  12|             • We are fully staffed for the first time in over a year. Our new Principal Assessor was hired in March 2024. A Data Collector
  13|             was added in September and the Assessor's Admin role was filled in November.
  14|             • As a result, we have been able to resume a full calendar of reviews and inspections for new construction and building permits.
  15|             •  We actually have gone back to capture some of the permits for review and inspection that we were unable to review in CY2023
  16|             and CY2022.
  17|             • Even in the face of some delays from our consultants and technical difficulties with the financial system, FY2025 tax bills were
  18|             created and mailed on time.
  19|             We successfully lobbied the Town to improve several Exemption eligibility qualifications, including Age reduction for Seniors, As-
  20|             set limit increases, Reimbursement increases and automatic Cost of Living adjustments.
  21|             The Board of Assessors' efforts at restoring the trust of Lunenburg taxpayers include scheduling all our meetings during evenings
  22|             (with both PACC coverage and Zoom access), so everyone can participate more easily. The Board has also developed a Property
  23|             Tax program for use at the Senior Center. We created a guide for "How to Read Your Vision Property Card", that is available in
  24|             both hard-copy and via the internet. We continue to improve visibility and access by redesigning of the Web-site, including analyt-
  25|             ics and explanations provided the Massachusetts DOR / Division of Local Services, Massachusetts Association of Assessing Offic-
  26|             ers, and UMass among others. The office staff strives to provide exceptional service that the Lunenburg taxpayers deserve.

  15|                                                                                   Jeremy Pierce             Jack Biery
  16|               The Town of Lunenburg Building Department is Located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 Massachusetts Avenue.
  17|               The Building Department works to efficiently, effectively, and consistently ensure a safe, sustainable, and vibrant community
  18|               through effective oversight of construction and development activities by enforcing the Massachusetts state Building, Plumbing,
  19|               Gas, and Electrical Codes, The Architectural Access Board Regulations, and Lunenburg's Zoning Bylaw and Town Code. The
  20|               Building Department also plays a critical role in assisting business through the process of obtaining permits and ensuring compli-
  21|               ance with all relevant codes and regulations. A commitment to being accessible, responsive and to providing a high level of cus-
  22|               tomer service empowers us to be a partner the community, property owners, businesses, contractors, and developers can rely on
  23|               when needed. The Building Department maintains this commitment by:
  24|               • Ensuring the public has consistent access to effective and courteous assistance in obtaining information, permits or approvals
  25|               related to construction, building or zoning regulations and providing clear information about regulations, permitting requirements
  26|               and associated fees.
  27|               • Maintaining a consistent level of communication by responding to all inquiries and applications in a timely manner.
  28|               Enforcing building codes, regulations, local codes, and safety standards to guarantee the safety of occupants and the community.
  29|               • Improving and optimizing the permitting process to facilitate efficient and timely approvals for construction projects while
  30|               maintaining thorough and consistent review procedures.
  31|               • Utilizing technology to streamline internal processes, enhance data management, and provide better online services for appli-
  32|               cants and property owners.
  33|               • Regularly reviewing and assessing departmental processes and policies, seeking opportunities for improvement, efficiency,
  34|              and effectiveness in serving the community.
  35|               • Coordinating with other departments to ensure a streamlined approach to permitting and enforcement as needed.
  36|                 eceiving and evaluating feedback from the public and other interested parties, and implementing changes as needed to ensur
  37|                 le department goals are being me

===PAGE 42=== (ocr, 26 lines)
   1|                                                                    CEMETERY COMMISSION
   2|                The Cemetery Commission and DPW Director, William Bernard met throughout the year to discuss cem-
   3|                etery business. Projects that were unfolded and being reviewed this year include the surveying of the open
   4|                field at North Cemetery; projects with the existing historical walls that surround North Cemetery; histori-
   5|                cal and Veterans gravesites that need care and repositioning. Review of diagrams of healthy trees that will
   6|                be kept in place around the grave-sites for future burials. The Cemetery Commission looks forward in the
   7|                upcoming months to begin discussing the project with the DPW and Engineering Firm to expand for buri-
   8|                als, well into the future at the North Cemetery.
   9|                The Cemetery Commission is looking toward a long-term solution for the boundary of the North Ceme-
  10|                tery along Holman Street. The Commissioners would like to thank the following people and businesses
  11|                whose help was acknowledged and appreciated.
  12|                The Cemetery Commission appreciated the efforts from the DPW Director, William Bernard, and Ceme-
  13|                tery Superintendent, Todd Holman who works toward maintaining the Lunenburg Cemeteries. Pete
  14|                Schmidt, James Versoi, Autumn Buck and Nick Green for the dedication and hard work they put in early
  15|                in the year, maintaining the Lunenburg Cemeteries. Dave Passios for his volunteer efforts and dedication
  16|                in keeping the well pumps in excellent working order. Leominster Monument Co. for their continued as-
  17|                sistance whenever it is needed. Dillis and Roy for their Engineering knowledge and completing the survey
  18|                for future grave-sites within the North Cemetery.
  19|                A special "thank you" for Lakeview Landscaping in planning, maintaining, the flowers that decorate the
  20|                entrances to both cemeteries.
  21|                The Cemetery Commission maintains an online mapping system for both cemeteries that can be found at:
  22|                www.burialsearch.com
  23|                Nancy Foss- Yasko, Chair
  24|                David Dorren, Vice- Chair
  25|                Michael Clark, Clerk

===PAGE 44=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                                                                               CONSERVATION
   2|              The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven-member commission, each appointed
   3|              to 3-year terms by the Select Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission
   4|              Act MGL chapter 40 s 8C. The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act
   5|              MGL chapter 131 s 40 and Chapter 239 of the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands. The Com-
   6|              mission also manages, and controls town owned lands retained for conservation and passive recrea-
   7|               tion use.
   8|              The Fiscal Year 2024-25 was a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   9|              meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single
  10|              family upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals. The Commission also held
  11|              numerous inspections on weekends prior to meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming
  12|              proposals that would appear on the agenda. The Conservation Administrator no longer attends site
  13|              walks with the Commissioners which has affected communications. During this period the Conserva-
  14|              tion Commission supported (numbers may be inaccurate due to lack of unified tracking system) 25
  15|              NOIs, 42 Administrative Determinations, 15 RDAs, 69 COCs, and numerous other requests.
  16|              The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans developed using
  17|              grants as outlined in the last annual reports. This management effort recently completed at the Rayno
  18|              property on Northfield Road resulting in $28,248 deposited into the Timber-Rights fund. The funding
  19|              from the sale of harvested timber provides the seed money to make it possible for the Commission to
  20|              apply for grants to maintain and improve Lunenburg's Conservation Lands. The Conservation Com-
  21|              mission continues to execute on the awarded MASS Recreational Trail Program Grant that was
  22|              awarded in 2023. The purpose of this grant was to fund the engineering design and permitting work
  23|              to create parking access in Table Rock and Rayno Conservation Areas. The grant scope also covered
  24|              improving Accessible Access to the Cowdrey Conservation Area as accessed from Townsend Harbor
  25|              Road. In early 2025, the permits were issued for Table Rock and Rayno Areas. This effort is ongoing
  26|              to complete the engineering design and permitting work in the Cowdrey.
  27|              The Pollinator Habitat portion of the Hollis Rd property provides for a diversity of native flowers
  28|              throughout the growing season. The Habitat promotes insect egg laying and nesting sites for pollina-
  29|              tors. The Habitat provides food sources for birds and numerous insects including native bees and but-
  30|              terflies. This unique "Hidden Gem" works as an ambassador for native pollinators to our community.
  31|              We are very thankful for the dedicated volunteers supporting the Lunenburg Community Pollinator
  32|              Habitat.
  33|                                                                                               44

   1|             In April 2025 the Conservation Commission sponsored April Earth Days projects in two Conserva-
   2|             tion Properties. The Pollinator habitat hosted a Season Kick-off which was supported by appx 17 vol-

   4|             & Trail building product in the Table Rock Conservation Area. The Table Rock project was support-
   5|             ed by 26 Volunteers. The Table Rock project contributed 132-man hours. The combined efforts of
   6|             our many volunteers during the Earth Day Events contributed $6,104 in "In-kind" donation (based on
   7|             Mass Trails $40.97 "in-kind rate) to our town. In years past, the Conservation Commission has spon-

   9|             tor volunteer availability. The Commission has almost completed "You Are Here" trail map signs at
  10|             trail heads and trail intersections to expand the safe use of Lunenburg's existing trail network. Com-
  11|             mission would like to express our gratitude for all the hard-working volunteers that work so enthusi-

  15|             give a better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to ensure contin-

   1|                                                                             COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|              The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all residents
   3|              age 50+ through supportive services, programs, and education. Councils on Aging were established
   4|              under Massachusetts General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the admin-
   5|              istration of elder programs or services and to develop, coordinate, and conduct such activities which
   6|              advocate for this population's issues.
   7|              The FY24 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Michele Beauvais, Adminis-
   8|              trative Assistant and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Pamela Myllykangas, Meal
   9|              Site Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Asst. Meal Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann Penney, Meal
  10|              Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, & Kimberly Moore, MART Van Drivers.
  11|              The Council on Aging Board is an eleven-member board appointed to 3-year terms by the Lunenburg
  12|              Select Board. The FY24 Council on Aging Board members were Chairperson Deb Lincoln,
  13|              Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Ana Lockwood, Pete Lincoln, Jim McGuigan, Diane Nowd, Cathy
  14|              Beale, Susan O'Sullivan, Elena Christopher, Judith Tarbell & Helen Balcom.
  15|              The Town budget alone does not support the Lunenburg COA at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Cen-
  16|              ter. Other sources of funding include the Eagle House Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, For-
  17|              mula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit
  18|              Authority.
  19|              In FY23 the Eagle House Senior Community Center rebranded its name to the Lunenburg Adult Ac-
  20|              tivity Center. The change has encouraged all people aged 50+ to visit the center and participate in
  21|              programming. FY24 showed an increase in the number of daily participants.
  22|              Construction was completed on a new ADA compatible patio/recreation/pavilion area at the center in
  23|              FY24. Funding was obtained through a $100,000 State Earmark, $100,000 in town capital funding,
  24|              $7500 Community Foundation Grant, and approximately $63,099 in donations to the Council on Ag-
  25|              ing Gift Fund. This outdoor space has been able to provide additional services and programs for the
  26|              community.
  27|              We are incredibly lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Council on Aging. In FY24, we had
  28|              fifty-two (52) volunteers who contributed 1726 hours of their time to help us out by doing things,
  29|              such as driving for the Meals on Wheels program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café,
  30|              office help, library, Discovery Center navigators, and friendly visitors/phone calls. We greatly appre-
  31|              ciate their service.
  32|              In FY24, we offered a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, congregate lunches and
  33|              breakfast programs, transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts, bocce, cards, and games, cul-
  34|              tural, Veterans Breakfast, movies, Historical Society presentations, information and referral, Memory
  35|              Café, health insurance counseling, and social events both at the Adult Activity Center and offsite to
  36|              individuals age 50t.

  25|             that support science and nature education, performing arts, and townwide cultural enrichment. We ac-
  26|             complish this through annual allocation of state grants for local projects and by producing cultural
  27|             events using locally-raised funds. We are committed to nurturing our artistic community through sup-
  28|             porting events that are diverse, accessible, collaborative, and fun for Lunenburg residents and visitors of
  29|             all ages with priority given to in-person, live events.
  30|             The Lunenburg Cultural Council, with grants from the Massachusetts Cultural Council, has funded 19
  31|             art, cultural, music, and science programs for the benefit of Lunenburg residents and members of the
  32|             surrounding community in 2024.

   1|                                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|             The Department of Public Works is made up of the Highway, Cemetery, Sewer, and Vehicle Maintenance Divisions.
   3|             The Department maintains roads, bridges, sewer infrastructure, and provides services essential to the quality of life and
   4|             welfare of the citizens of Lunenburg. The staff consists of one Director, one Executive Assistant (shared with the Facili-
   5|             ties Department), one Highway Superintendent, 3 Heavy Equipment Operators, 2 Driver/Laborers, one Mechanic, one
   6|             Cemetery Superintendent, two Seasonal Cemetery Laborers, one Sewer Business Manager, and one Assistant to the
   7|             Sewer Business Manager.
   8|             Departmental Mission:
   9|             The mission of the Department of Public Works is to provide high quality, cost effective, efficient, and safe services to
  10|             protect, preserve and improve the Town's roadway infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and en-
  11|             hances the current and future social and economic needs of the community; to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality
  12|             environment for the Town's citizens and visitors; and to provide environmentally sound means of solid waste and
  13|             wastewater disposal.
  14|             Departmental Goals and Objectives
  15|             Be prepared to respond to any weather, roadway infrastructure, or accident-related emergency that may arise on a 24
  16|             hour per day, three hundred and sixty-five day per year basis.
  17|             Maintain and enhance a highly qualified and dedicated staff of public works professionals.
  18|             Provide preventative maintenance in a proactive manner to reduce reactive maintenance as follows:
  19|                        Maintain and update the sewer system and associated pumping stations.
  20|                         Maintain and repair roadways per the 2022 pavement management plan.
  21|                        Maintain and repair sidewalks per the 2020 PROW ADA Transition plan.
  22|                        Increase the number of roadsides mowed by developing a roadside maintenance plan.
  23|                        Increase the number of catch basin and stormwater structures cleaned and the number of miles of roadways
  24|                              swept per the June 2020 Operations and Maintenance Plan.
  25|                        Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans for re-
  26|                              habilitation.
  27|             Increase training offered by our insurance provider to obtain credits to lessen premiums.
  28|             Find new sources (Federal, State, and other grants) of funding to supplement the operational budget.
  29|             Continue to maintain a high level of courteous communication and productive interaction between members of boards
  30|             and commissions as well as Town residents.
  31|             Strive to modernize the operations of the Department by obtaining and using the most up to date equipment and meth-
  32|             ods, including asset management.
  33|             Highway Division
  34|             The highway division provided daily maintenance and repairs of roads, drainage, signs, trees, roadsides, sewers, and
  35|             more. They assisted all other Departments as requested and responded to emergencies 24 hours per day as needed. The
  36|             DPW Director reviewed and approved applications for permits for Road Openings, Street Improvements, Trench Safety,
  37|             Sewer Connections, and Sewer Extensions. The Director reviewed applications for driveway installations, site plans,
  38|             stormwater BMP's, building demolition, and sign installations for DPW related issues.
  39|             Pavement Management
  40|             The DPW continued to pave and preserve roadways per the 2022 Pavement Management Plan. The following roads
  41|             were paved with hot mix asphalt this year:
  42|                         • Leominster-Shirley • Pioneer Drive                               • Fort Pond Road
  43|                              Road                                                                                            : Went CooteRdad Elmwood Road

===PAGE 50=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                   DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|               Storm Water
   3|               Working to stay in compliance with our EPA NPDES MS4 Permit continues to still be an important function of the
   4|               DPW. Along with the Storm Water Task Force, and our Storm Water Consultant, the DPW works to meet the obliga-
   5|               tions of the permit and reporting requirements each year. The DPW provides maintenance and care of the Town's storm
   6|               water management systems. This includes, but is not limited to catch-basin cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting, re-
   7|               placing pipes, swale cleaning, and maintaining Town owned stormwater BMP's. The Department responds to calls for
   8|               clogged catch-basins and culverts to help control ponding and flooding. Storm water related issues continue to rise.
   9|               Using Fiscal Year 2023 Capital Funds, the failed culvert on Page Street at Arbor Street was replaced by RA Powell Con-
  10|               struction with a 48-inch concrete pipe with precast concrete headwalls.
  11|               Tree Removal
  12|               A Tree Risk Management Inventory of almost 1,200 trees on the roads in Lunenburg was completed in 2015. The inven-
  13|               tory prioritizes the removal and/or pruning of the trees. However, storm damage and natural attrition creates a backlog
  14|               of tree work that exceeds the Department's allotted budget for tree removal. The Department strives to fulfill each resi-
  15|               dent's request for tree-work but must prioritize work based upon the determined degree of danger to person and property
  16|               and location. The DPW continued to act in a reactionary manner as needs arose. All tree-work other than brush remov-
  17|               al, chipping, and the removal of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the lack of a bucket truck,
  18|               crane, and a licensed tree professional. Each spring and after storms, the DPW staff remove and dispose of woody de-
  19|               bris within the Town's right-of-way. An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of the
  20|               Town by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community. The DPW appreci-
  21|               ates and thanks Unitil for their responsiveness in removing hazardous trees when asked to do so.
  22|               Roadside Vegetation Maintenance
  23|               The DPW staff responded to many requests to cut and clear overgrown vegetation from roadsides. This work was most-
  24|               ly done using our new utility tractor with a boom rotary mower attachment (22-foot horizontal reach and 19.5-foot verti-
  25|               cal reach). Some of this work continues to me completed using lawn mowers, saws, and other hand tools. The DPW is
  26|               developing a town-wide program of selective curbside mowing and vegetation clearing using the new tractor and boom
  27|               mower that will significantly enhance our roadside vegetation maintenance program.
  28|               Landfill
  29|               The closed and capped landfill off Youngs Road is inspected by a consultant semiannually and is still in compliance with
  30|               the requirements of the permit issued by the Massachusetts Division of Environmental Protection (DEP). Gas monitor-
  31|               ing and groundwater contamination sampling and testing is conducted by a consultant. The landfill site including the
  32|               perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel. The DPW hires a local contractor to mow the
  33|               cap per DEP Requirements.
  34|               Adjacent to the closed landfill is a storage area for catch-basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be haz-
  35|               ardous waste by the EPA. The DEP has issued a Beneficial Use Determination (BUD) permit which allows the storage
  36|               of these materials. A consultant performs the yearly testing of these materials.
  37|               On seven Saturdays in the fall and the spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage Yard Waste Days. Residents may
  38|               drop off brush, leaves, and other organic materials for composting.
  39|                                                                                               50

===PAGE 51=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|             Solid Waste
   3|             Solid waste (trash) was collected curbside weekly under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our vendor E.L.
   4|             Harvey & Sons, Inc. Recyclable materials were also collected curbside weekly by Harvey. Pay as You Throw bags may
   5|             be bought at Hannaford Supermarket, Smokestack Roasters, Maki Building Center, Honey Farms, Jaxx Country Variety,
   6|             Lunenburg Gulf, and Baker's Whalom Variety. The DPW remains the point of contact between residents and our trash
   7|             vendor.
   8|             Residents may dispose of household hazardous waste for a fee at the Devens Regional Household Hazardous Waste Col-
   9|             lection Center on published dates and times. All Lunenburg residents have access to more fee-based recycling services
  10|             at the CHARM center found at the Townsend Recycling Center at 33 Greenville Road, Townsend, MA.
  11|             Lake Shirley Dam
  12|             In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair, The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of the dam
  13|             to the Town. After the completion of the total reconstruction of the dam, the responsibility for the maintenance, moni-
  14|             toring, and record keeping were completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. Compliance
  15|             with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed
  16|             under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works in 2004-2005.                              The dam is inspected by a professional engineer
  17|             licensed in Massachusetts. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report, and the dam is still in compliance
  18|             with regulations.
  19|             Winter Operations
  20|             The DPW was prepared for and responded to all snow and ice weather events with the goal of providing a high level of
  21|             service in a timely manner while at the same time trying to minimize costs. Lunenburg joins with 17 other towns in a
  22|             cooperative bid to obtain the best possible pricing for rock salt. However, the price of rock salt continues to steadily in-
  23|             crease every year. The DPW maintains a small fleet of snow removal equipment (trucks, plows, spreaders, loader, back-
  24|             hoe, and sidewalk plow). The fleet is supplemented with vendors as needed. Due to breakdowns of the Town's aged
  25|             fleet of large dump trucks, more vendors were needed; thus, adding to costs. Four new snow fighter trucks (large trucks
  26|             with a salt spreader, front plow, and wing plow) have been ordered and are expected to be available in Fiscal Years 2025
  27|             and 2026.
  28|             The clearing of snow and ice from roadways is a critical operation of the DPW as it is a matter of public safety.
  29|             The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private vendors who tirelessly
  30|             and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of the severity or duration of a storm. "DPW work-
  31|             ers are the original Storm Troopers."
  32|             Cemetery
  33|             The maintenance of the Town's cemeteries was performed by the Cemetery Division Superintendent, and two seasonal
  34|             cemetery laborers. The DPW thanks the Massachusetts Department of Corrections for providing crews to help with
  35|             maintenance. The Superintendent coordinates burials with families and funeral homes and meets regularly with the three
  36|             -member Cemetery Commission. The Highway Division provides equipment and staff for maintenance and burials,
  37|             which on average is forty per year. The DPW thanks, Lakeview Landscaping, Dillis and Roy Civil Design Group, Le-
  38|             ominster Monument, Vitelli Monument, Lori Rivers, Lunenburg Dipping Donuts, and Tomatoes Pizza for their service
  39|             to the cemeteries this year.

===PAGE 53=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                   FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|             The Lunenburg Finance Committee is pleased to report that Lunenburg's financial position is solid. In planning for Fis-
   3|             cal Year 2024, the Town has continued the sound, conservative management practices from prior years and has produced
   4|             a budget that is responsible and maintains appropriate funding for the needs of all departments and Town residents.
   5|             The process of developing and presenting a budget to Annual Town Meeting begins in the early fall with the Town Man-
   6|             ager meeting with department heads to discuss expenses, wants, and needs. The Town Manager then presents the pro-
   7|             posed budget to the Finance Committee in mid-February. Over the following 90 days, the Finance Committee meets
   8|             with the various department heads to discuss their individual line item budget requests. The Finance Committee then
   9|             votes on the recommendation of the finalized omnibus budget provided by the Town Manager and reports to Annual
  10|             Town Meeting. As the Legislative body acting on the Town's behalf, the Finance Committee recommends the approval
  11|             of the budget as presented at Annual Town Meeting.
  12|             Historically, the Capital Plan has been funded though free cash, taxes revenues, and unspent capital appropriation from
  13|             prior years. For FY24, the available free cash is substantially lower due to the need to fill the overlay account with
  14|             enough funds to offset a worst-case scenario with resident tax abatements. Without the use of ARPA funds, the proposed
  15|             Capital Plan would fund $1,039,010, a 52% decrease from FY23. With the inclusion of the ARPA funds, the proposed
  16|             Capital Plan funds $2,025,010, a 6.4% decrease from FY23. This conservative approach allows the Town to fund critical
  17|             items in the Capital Plan while reserving enough money to cover potential abatements.
  18|             Understandably, an important issue on the minds of many residents revolves around the current assessments and subse-
  19|             quent tax levy. While the personal positions, understanding, and potential outcomes of the taxpayers may widely vary,
  20|             there are a fair number of certainties.
  21|                                                            FY22 Final                    FY23 Final                 FY24 Budgeted              FY22/FY23       FY23/FY24
  22|                                                                                                                                                 % Change         % Change
  23|                   Prior Year Levy Limit              $     26,049,570.00            $ 27,131,063.00                $ 28,043,722.60                4.15%             3.36%
  24|                        22% Increase                   $         651,239.25          $         678,276.58          $         701,093.07            4.15%             3.36%
  25|                 Estimated New Growth                  $        430,254.00            $        234,383.77          $         200,000.00           -45.52%          -14.67%
  26|                       Debt Exclusion                 $       2,828,812.36           $      2,831,932.29            $   2,477,704.00               0.11%           -12.51%
  27|                         Taxes: Total                 $   29,959,875.61              $ 30,875,655.64               $ 31,477,704.00                 3.06%            1.95%
  28|             1. The increases felt by some taxpayers do not indicate a windfall of cash for the Town. Lunenburg is restricted by
  29|                   Proposition 2½ (Mass. Gen. L. c. 59, § 21C) and, as such, is unable to raise the overall levy of the Town by any
  30|                  more that 2½% of the previous year's levy limit plus any New Growth and Excludible Debt. The table above out-
  31|                  lines the changes in taxes, showing the modest increase from year to year.
  32|                 Many Town residents have filed abatements, disputing the assessments made of their homes. Should all abatements
  33|                  be granted, the Town would be short of revenue by approximately $1,500,000. To best cover this potential outcome,
  34|                  the Town Manager has recommended the transfer of $1,500,000 from Free Cash to the Overlay Account.
  35|             3. The abatement process and Overlay Account are the proper methods of both addressing the issue and reversing the
  36|                   financial burden places on some of the Town residents.
  37|                                                                                               53

===PAGE 54=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|             Noteworthy topics:
   2|             • The end of FY23 will see a 14.56% reduction of our Debt Service and Excludible Debt, from $3,174,257.53 (FY23)
   3|                  to $2,712,069.90 (FY24). This decrease is a result of the retiring of excludible debt from the Public Safety Building
   4|                   and the Primary School as well as a completed MSBA reimbursement for the Primary School. As a result, debt ca-
   5|                  pacity is made available for future projects. An analysis, run by Unibank, projected a variety of scenarios to guide
   6|                   the Town in debt capacity spending over the next 30 years. In total, the Town has $42 million that will be useable
   7|                  while remaining under debt capacity limits. With this number in mind, the Select Board, Municipal Building Design
   8|                   Committee, and the Town will be better prepared to plan for the future.
   9|                Federal relief, during the COVID-19 pandemic, provided Lunenburg with $3,500,000 in ARPA funds and
  10|                  $1,900,000 in ESSER funds. ARPA funding is to be spent at the discretion of the Select Board. In an attempt to min-
  11|                  imize the delivery time, the Select Board voted to spend $986,000 in ARPA funds to order three items from the Cap-
  12|                   ital Plan; a replacement Rescue Ambulance ($506,000) for the Fire Department and two-6 wheel dump trucks
  13|                  ($240,000 each) for the DPW. Additionally, the Select Board has committed $150,000 to the tax assessment investi-
  14|                  gation as well as approximately $38,000 for security upgrades on the Public Safety Building. The remaining
  15|                   $2,200,000 has yet to be allocated as the Select Board considers recommendations from various department heads
  16|                  and Town citizens. ESSER funds allowed the School Department to hire 13 new positions across the district. Social
  17|                   workers, guidance counselors, subject specialists, tutors, and technicians were all brought into the school to provide
  18|                  much needed support to the Town's youth. The School Department will need to look for additional funding or other
  19|                  ways to maintain the new staff as the last of the ESSER funding is required to be spent by the end of September
  20|                  2024.
  21|             • Cherry Sheet offsets and other State monies have historically, on average, underwhelmed the Town. While FY23 did
  22|                  see a larger than expected increase (11.5%) in Chapter 70 (public school funding) funds, the outlook for FY24 is the
  23|                   likely mandatory minimum increase resulting in a 0.6% increase. In FY23, the Ch. 70 increase was more than
  24|                  enough to cover the 20.4% increase in the Monty Tech assessment. For FY24, the 0.6% increase will not be enough
  25|                   to offset the 12% increase in the Monty Tech Assessment. The Finance Committee met with our State Delegation
  26|                   and pushed for a re-assessment of the formulas used to determine funding to small municipalities.
  27|             The Finance Committee wishes to thank the Town Manager, Town Accountant, Capital Planning Committee, Select
  28|             Board, and all the department heads that gave their time and effort to produce the budget presented at Annual Town
  29|             Meeting. We recommend passage of the Town Manager's FY24 omnibus budget.
  30|                   Respectfully,
  31|                   The Lunenburg Finance Committee: Evan Watters, Chair; Christopher Menard, Vice-Chair; Thomas Balish,
  32|                   Clerk; Jay Simone; Thomas Gray; Caesar Nuzzolo
  33|                                                                                              54

   1|                                                                       HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|             Richard McGrath, Chair, LHC
   3|             Jennifer Sanderson, Member
   4|             Kevin Boussom, Member
   5|             The Lunenburg Historical Commission continued to follow its State Charter by advocating for the town's pre-
   6|             cious, and dwindling, historic assets. The Commission did this through facilitating the 18 month demolition
   7|             delay bylaw and having regular communications with the Building Commissioner and the Architectural
   8|             Preservation District Commission.
   9|             The Commission, in conjunction with the Massachusetts Historical Commission and Brian Knight Research,
  10|             has an ongoing town wide survey in progress. The survey includes eighty five properties, most of which are on
  11|             the 1833 Cyrus Kilburn map of Lunenburg. Marshall Park will also be included in the survey.
  12|             Demolition delay has been instituted for 763 Massachusetts Avenue with no end day in effect due to noncom-
  13|             pliance by the owner. The commission is restoring the Goodrich Family cemetery plot. The reproduction of the
  14|             Levi Goodridge, desecrated and badly damaged headstone, is nearly complete and is slated to be set in the
  15|             Spring or Summer of 2025. The other existing headstones will be cleaned and preserved. The commission is
  16|             working on a Hometown Heroes Banner program honoring the town's veterans and hopes to have it running in
  17|             2025.
  18|             There are only 3 commissioners of 5 needed to run the commission properly. The lack of a full commission
  19|             leads to many meetings not having a quorum. This has been an ongoing problem and severely limits the pro-
  20|             gress of our work.
  21|             Richard McGrath, Chair

   1|                                                        Lunenburg Public Library
   2|                            "Books permit us to voyage through time, to tap the wisdom of our ancestors. The li-
   3|                            brary connects us with the insight and knowledge, painfully extracted from Nature, of
   4|                            the greatest minds that ever were, with the best teachers, drawn from the entire planet
   5|                            and from all our history, to instruct us without tiring, and to inspire us to make our
   6|                            own contribution to the collective knowledge of the human species. I think the health
   7|                            of our civilization, the depth of our awareness about the underpinnings of our culture
   8|                            and our concern for the future can all be tested by how well we support our libraries. -
   9|                            Carl Sagan
  10|             The only way to start the Library's report this year is to acknowledge the loss of one of our trustees. Robert "Bob"
  11|             Conroy served for years as Secretary of the Library Board of Trustees, and was one of the most active users of the li-
  12|             brary. He was unassuming and impactful, and he brought thoughtfulness, joy, and care to the library community. He will
  13|             be missed. The recent success of the library is in no small way a result of his activity and passion.
  14|             Similarly, all six of our other Library Trustees work tirelessly behind the scenes to ensure that the Lunenburg Public Li-
  15|             brary is a truly special place for the community to gather and visit. Although the conclusion of year was darkened by this
  16|             loss, the library saw a very successful year.
  17|             The library is fully staffed for the first time in more than two years, and you will see many new faces next time you visit.
  18|             All three of our young library pages are brand new employees, their predecessors leaving to pursue their studies, and we
  19|             filled on vacancy with a new Young Adult Services Librarian, Kaitlyn Himmelberger, and our other vacancy with a new
  20|             Digital Services and Outreach Librarian, Melanie King.
  21|             Staff members new and established dedicate their work to the organization's mission:
  22|             Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community, enrich-
  23|             ing our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
  24|             This focus has contributed to one of our most successful years in memory. Visitors increased by 7% this year, circulation
  25|             of physical items rose 8% to almost 78,000 items, and the usage of the library's electronic materials (e-books, e-
  26|             audiobooks, and online databases) increased for the fourth year in a row to record highs. In the last two years, we have
  27|             added seven new databases to our collection.
  28|             Our library is full of activity and our meeting spaces are in high demand. Library staff offer at least one program for the
  29|             public nearly every day we are open. More than 6,100 people attended library programs this past year. Community
  30|             groups, including boy and girl scouts, local Ham Radio operators, elected boards and committees, the Lunenburg Boys
  31|             and Girls Club, and homeschooling groups, use our facility to support their activities. Crowds of teenagers gather at the
  32|             library after school and hundreds of people have signed up to participate in our Summer Reading Program in 2024.
  33|             We also made several administrative changes to our staffing this year. First, we successfully reclassified one of our Staff
  34|             Librarians as Assistant Director to reflect the tasks and responsibilities that this position performs. Second, we expanded
  35|             our Digital Services Librarian position to include responsibility in Outreach Services, giving us a designated coordinator
  36|             for these increasingly important services. The funding for this change came from a vacant Library Assistant position that
  37|             will not be filled. These changes are contributed to successful recruitment and retention of key employees.
  38|             In addition to those changes, our Director and Assistant Director have developed a staffing plan to help support the de-
  39|             velopment of our employees. Developing our own workers allows us to build resilience and should aid in retaining our
  40|             skilled and dedicated library staff.

   1|                                                          Lunenburg Public Library
   2|             One of the more exciting changes on which we are working is a renovation to our Teen Room. In a facility that is full of
   3|             remarkably forward looking decisions for the 2005 era, the inclusion of a space dedicated to serving the teen demograph-
   4|             ic is vitally important. Unfortunately, the space is small for the number of young people we see. Expanding this space
   5|             without changing the overall footprint of the building will bring a substantive and beneficial change to the library. Alt-
   6|             hough we do not anticipate construction until FY 2025, planning is well underway and staff and trustees and exciting to
   7|             reveal these improvements.
   8|             The Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library have also funded a significant facelift for the front of the building. You
   9|             will see a sign clearly labelling the building and legible from Massachusetts Avenue. The sign successfully follows the
  10|             design language of the architect while it adorns the front entrance of the facility.
  11|             The first full year of separate investment for the Library Trust Funds was also a remarkable success. Following the pru-
  12|             dent investor rule, we have successfully increased the total in trust from $89,070 to $97,437 this year. Some of these
  13|             funds originated in the 1800's as gifts, and the trustees began a more direct stewardship of these funds at the end FY
  14|             2023.
  15|             Adult Services Notes

  18|             nity groups coming to the library to use the meeting rooms. Other highlights include:
  19|             An increase in popularity for the quarterly puzzle swaps, which averaged 50-60 attendees and 450-500 puzzles at each

  22|             brary, as the year's music concerts averaged an attendance of 34 people; five more than average the preceding year.

  25|             • A local guitar instructor hosted Saturday morning music clinics once a month that open to anyone. 35 attendees
  26|                  joined us over the four sessions held in the library.

  30|                   ing containers to plant at home in the springtime.
  31|             • One of the local apiaries came in the spring to share honey samples and their expertise on beekeeping with 9 interest-
