   1|                                                               LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   2|             FY24 Mission: Establish, develop, modernize, and operate the Town of Lunen-
   3|             burg's Public, Government, and Educational (PEG)Access Channels to reflect
   4|             Lunenburg
   5|             Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   6|             Steve Walker, Chair
   7|             Faith Beall, Vice Chair
   8|             Steve Archambault, Secretary
   9|             Pete Lincoln, Member
  10|             Paul Sarapas, Member
  11|             The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the Public
  12|             Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town's only cable-service provider.
  13|             The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast
  14|             programming. Original content is also conceived and produced by PAC. Town events/meetings and school activi-
  15|             ties, as well as outside sources of programming, are all provided to increase the viewer's knowledge of what is hap-
  16|             pening around town.
  17|             Programming
  18|             CH8 - Government
  19|             Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcasted meetings. In addition to several live weekly
  20|             municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other government programs downloaded from MassAccess MMX
  21|             (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a political nature. There was
  22|             an increase in local government meetings being recorded and uploaded to our government and YouTube channels,
  23|             including Cemetery Commission, Board of Assessors, Personnel Committee and Master Plan Steering Committee.
  24|             This increase in FY24 of Government programming resulted in more committee and event recording requests. The
  25|             additional programming allowed residents to view meetings they normally wouldn't see. And, added in FY24 from
  26|             another local station, Fitchburg Access Television (FATV) was FLAPtv, a political opinion program focusing on
  27|             Fitchburg and Leominster area politics.
  28|             More Government meeting availability has created easy access to a greater number of board and committee meet-
  29|             ings for the community.
  30|             CH9 - Public
  31|             Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 78% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY24, up 6% from FY23. The total
  32|             number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (outside resources), increased from 109 in FY23 to
  33|             125 programs in FY24 resulting in an increase of overall programs of 14%.
  34|             Ninety-seven (78%) local programs included high school sports, band concerts, school events and featured projects.
  35|             The remaining 28 (22%) programs were obtained through shared media sources.
  36|             Two of the top three program categories, Informational and Events (51%) provided viewers with presentations and
  37|             overviews that helped to familiarize them with resources and information, as well as current events happening
  38|             around town.
  39|                                                                                               60

   1|                                                                                                           As a member of the MassAccess organization, PAC
   2|             MASSOACCESS is given aces to the elve Connect                                                 (massaccess.telvue.com) (MMX) database for shar-
   3|                                                                                                          ing productions of both state and nationwide pro-
   4|             gramming. Many shows downloaded and aired on Channel 9 and Ch22 are sourced from this outlet, including
   5|             "CONNECT: Addressing Loneliness in Massachusetts."
   6|             In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess Telvue Connect content data-
   7|             base, or MMX. The results were outstanding! PAC has 102 Affiliates on the database and growing. In FY24, Lunen-
   8|             burg's "Talkin' Baseball" episodes were, and continue to be, downloaded by over 40 media centers around the coun-
   9|             try. "Lunenburg Storytime - The Books We Love" was downloaded by over 80 public access stations. Most stations
  10|             that downloaded our programs are in Massachusetts but reach as far as California. We are delighted to showcase pro-
  11|             grams to the community while using the database to add to PAC content.
  12|             In addition to MassAccess Telvue (MMX) downloads, PAC also receives local programs from nearby access stations
  13|             that air on Ch9, including "Discussing Fitchburg" and "North Central Journal."
  14|             ARPA FUNDS
  15|             In FY23, the Town of Lunenburg received ARPA funding. PAC presented an outline of potential spending to the Select
  16|             Board, to be awarded a portion of the available funding. The PAC request was in three parts: Videographer, Broadcast
  17|             and Studio. The Videographer section included the purchase of products such as audio recorders and portable lighting to
  18|             better equip the videographers. Improvement of the Bilotta room audio was the intent of the Broadcast section with a
  19|             focus on new hall speakers. And, the Studio part targeted significant improvements to the current studio, including au-
  20|             dio and broadcast equipment, as well as set furniture.
  21|             PAC's proposal for ARPA funding was approved for $50,700, the full amount requested.
  22|             In FY24 PAC focused ARPA spending on videographer and studio equipment categories. Over twenty-one thousand
  23|             dollars was used to purchase items including studio cameras, microphones, camera bags, lighting and a studio switch-
  24|             er. By using these funds, PAC was able to buy much needed equipment not available in the budget.
  25|             Studio and Equipment
  26|             PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take advantage of the facili-
  27|             ties and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various town departments in the
  28|             use of public access' resources for their communication needs.
  29|             In FY24 PAC made a change in studio broadcasting equipment, from using a TriCaster Mini to purchasing a Blackmagic
  30|             ATEM TV Studio Switcher. With ARPA funding, PAC was able to do the upgrade, which also included the purchase of
  31|             3 Blackmagic studio cameras. The shows created with these products were markedly improved in quality.
  32|                                                                                               62

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|             Staff
   2|             In FY24, there was one full-time Manager, a Production Technician, 4 part-time videographers and a part-time contract-
   3|             ed Bulletin Administrator. There were openings for 2 part-time videographers.
   4|              The PAC Manager administers the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all production and programming
   5|             schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, social
   6|             media and equipment and system troubleshooting. Videographers provide video recording of committee meetings and
   7|             are available for local video assignments and projects.
   8|              The Production Technician manages the day-to-day operations of the studio and assists the videographers with training
   9|             and equipment needs. Reporting to the Manager, he provides technical assistance for production and studio sup-
  10|             port. The team relies on the Production Technician to oversee and assist with troubleshooting issues that arise. He also
  11|             advises on new equipment and supervises set up and administration of large productions.
  12|             PAC's 4 Videographers work 2-10 hours per week recording board and committee meetings, sports, events and special
  13|             projects. They work "in the field" as well as in the studio producing such shows as "Talkin' Baseball" and "Movie
  14|              Talk." The staff responds to recording requests from town officials and management on a regular basis and help cover
  15|             larger productions such as the LHS home football games and Town Meetings, resulting in high quality 4 camera produc-
  16|              tions.
  17|             PAC is always looking for producers. If a community member has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the
  18|              show happen. Our studio and staff are equipped to assist new producers in video production.
  19|             Financials

  22|              Summary
  23|              FY24 was a year of growth for PAC. While our shared programming on the MMX database steadily grew, the lo-
  24|              cal programming offered on Ch9 also increased by 14% over FY23. As a result, PAC offered more informational
  25|              and event programs to the community. These programs were popular on our stations, as well as on the Lunenburg
  26|              Access YouTube channel. Viewers were able to access a wide variety of shows, including health updates and LHS
  27|              sports. More content was created with minimal staff, as videographers juggled multiple commitments to deliver
  28|             informative programming.
  29|                                                                                               64

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 28 lines)
   1|             PAC staff is the core of the group. The team is supportive and dedicated to the goals of the organization.
   2|              Videographers are always learning new skills and training on new equipment. This was the case in FY24, as PAC
   3|             upgraded the broadcast equipment in the studio. They also continually cover hybrid board and committee meetings
   4|              as requested without hesitation. There are currently over 20 board and committee meetings that are recorded either
   5|             through Zoom or in hybrid mode. The Cemetery Commission was added this year, as well as the Board of Asses-
   6|             sors meetings. While short staffed, PAC videographers have managed to keep up with government recordings and
   7|              local shows of interest.
   8|              As ARPA had been allocated in FY23, PAC planned spending very carefully utilizing funding to best benefit the
   9|             community. The focus in FY24 was on videographer equipment and studio production. The upgrade to the Bilotta
  10|             room audio is scheduled for FY25. Nearly half of the approved funds for PAC ($50,700) were spent in FY24 giv-
  11|             ing videographer greater resources and skills to do their jobs. With the help of the Production Technician, products
  12|              were sourced and training was given on new equipment.
  13|              While funding from Comcast has been declining each quarter, PAC was able to surpass FY23 programming num-
  14|             bers in FY24 by 14%. This trend was largely due to careful planning and allocation of resources to obtain the best
  15|             results. With the assistance of ARPA funds, PAC was able to improve videographers' tools, while upgrading stu-
  16|              dio broadcast equipment offering expanded programming. The consistent increase in government meetings contin-
  17|             ued through FY24, as new committees were added both live and through Zoom. All local programs and meetings
  18|             continue to be available on the Lunenburg Access YouTube channel. Overall, PAC had an excellent year of growth
  19|              producing shows of interest that serve the local community.

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                                                     SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                                  JANUARY 1, 2024 - NOVEMBER 1, 2024
   3|             The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is administered by the Assistant Town Manager/HR Director.
   4|              What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off!
   5|              The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to have
   6|             an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs such as
   7|              general office work, custodial, library shelving, meals on wheels, school assistance, etc., to earn the tax abatement.
   8|              The 2024 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to the participant's 3rd quarter real estate tax bill.
   9|              The current hourly rate is $15.00 per hour.
  10|              Eligibility Requirements
  11|             • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  12|             • Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July Ist
  13|             • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name (60 years+)
  14|             • Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  15|                        one person household - $50,000
  16|                       two person household - $75,000
  17|              • Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  18|             • Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Re-
  19|                   tirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement
  20|             • Final amount earned will be applied to the 3rd quarter real estate tax
  21|             • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  22|             • All Property Tax Workers must re- apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program
  23|             2024 Participation
  24|              During 2024, there were nine residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:
  25|             • Building Department/Zoning
  26|             • Council on Aging
  27|             • Public Library
  28|              • Tax Collector
  29|              • Assessors
  30|             • Board of Health
  31|             These individuals as a whole, accumulated 640.50 hours amounting to $9,607.50, before deductions, in real estate
  32|             tax abatements.
  33|                                                                                               66

===PAGE 67=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|                                                                             SEWER COMMISSION
   2|             The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the fu-
   3|             ture, and developing and implementing rules, regulations, and policies. Under the Town's Charter, responsibil-
   4|             ity for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the
   5|             Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to
   6|             the Department of Public Works' Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant.
   7|             Approximately 84% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 16% is dis-
   8|             charged to the City of Fitchburg.
   9|             The FY24 Sewer Commission consisted of: John Reynolds-Chair, Brett Ramsden-Vice Chair, Mike Nault-
  10|             Clerk and Members Dave MacDonald and Mike Mackin. The Commission continued to meet virtually via
  11|             Zoom for all meetings and Paula Bertram continued as Sewer Business Manager.
  12|             Currently, there are 1,233 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 263 which have access to
  13|             Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY24, 21 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Com-
  14|             mission billed out $1,037,308 in quarterly sewer bills and had $35,189.72 of prior year delinquencies added to
  15|             the owners' property tax bills for non-payment.
  16|             An upgrade to the Munis system software in FY24 caused tremendous issues with creating the billing each
  17|             quarter. The upgrade also played havoc with calculations for demand billing for overdue accounts and pre-
  18|             vented the demands from going out. The delinquency rate for bills averaged 20-30% during the year. Paula
  19|             spent huge amounts of time working with IT Support and a Consultant to get the quarterly billing out.
  20|             DPW Director, Bill Bernard and Paula worked with the Commission to carry out the Maintenance Plan for the
  21|             pump stations. The department was awarded $100K in ARPA funds for manhole repairs to address I/I getting
  22|             into the system. The work will go out to bid for work in FY25.
  23|                                                                                               67

===PAGE 68=== (ocr, 29 lines)
   1|              The contract for Operation & Maintenance of the Sewer Stations went out to bid and CTE won the contract.
   2|              In September 2023, Robert Lilley took over as technician for maintaining the Sewer Stations and worked with
   3|              Paula to develop a capital plan for upgrading pumps and generators, in addition to other parts for the next sev-
   4|              eral years. Robert suggested cleaning all the wet wells yearly, along with needed major repairs at the Mass 1
   5|              and Dana St. stations.
   6|              During FY24, clogs at the pump stations became a major issue and were caused by rags and wet wipes getting
   7|              into the system. The Leominster Rd. station had 22 clogs in 7 months. The Commission sent out reminders to
   8|              users and placed notices in the paper to only flush waste and toilet paper down the system. Clogs jam the
   9|              pumps and can cause costly repairs.
  10|              In FY24, a sub-committee of the Commission, along with Bill and Paula, began negotiations of the Leominster
  11|              Inter-Municipal Agreement, which would expire in June 2024. After several meetings and drafts, the Agree-
  12|              ment was finalized in the Spring. Leominster will be increasing their rates by 16.17% in FY25 and an addi-
  13|              tional 3% in FY26. The Connection Fee for new hookups will be increased from $750 to $3,500. In addition,
  14|              Fitchburg will be increasing their rate by 7% each year for the next 5 years. These increases made it necessary
  15|              for the Commission to raise user rates by 16% in FY25.
  16|              At the 5/4/24 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 3 articles, 2 of which related to the FY24 budget
  17|              and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY25, $1,712,419.87 was appropriated from antici-
  18|              pated Sewer Enterprise receipts, Retained Earnings and transfer from available funds. The second article trans-
  19|              ferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $35,000 and is to be used to maintain
  20|              and improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. The third warrant
  21|              article was to transfer the sum of $1,500.00 into the OPEB Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town
  22|              Meeting.
  23|              Sewer Commission as of report submittal (Feb 2025):

===PAGE 69=== (ocr, 29 lines)
   1|                                                                           VETERANS' SERVICES
   2|             The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehensive system of assistance offered to Veterans,
   3|             their families and surviving spouses in all of the United States. Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accord-
   4|             ance with chapter 115 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must have available to its citizens a Veterans' Ser-
   5|             vices Officer (VSO), either full or part-time, as determined by the community population, in order to serve the
   6|             needs of its veterans. If they want to File VA Claims they must be State Certified thru the VA and are called
   7|             Veteran Agents. Lunenburg has a part-time Veteran Agent at the Ritter Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave.
   8|             The Office hours are Thursdays from 9:00 AM thru 12:00 PM.
   9|             Veterans, families, or widows who qualify can be provided monthly financial assistance (Chapter 115 Bene-
  10|             fits), which are reimbursements for specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharmacy
  11|             co-payments. With the Veteran Agent's approval, the Town of Lunenburg provides the financial assistance,
  12|             but then the town is reimbursed by the State's Department of Veterans' Services (DVS). It is the function of
  13|             the Veterans' Services Office to identify qualifying members of the community and assist with enrollment into
  14|             the DVS program. Additionally, certain Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from
  15|             the Veterans Administration (VA). Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
  16|             During Fiscal Year 2024 (July 1, 2023- June 30, 2024), Lunenburg is serving an average of 7 recipients
  17|             (Veterans, Families, or Widows) per month on Chapter 115. Lunenburg paid out $44,454.57 in benefits, or an
  18|             average of $3,704.55 per month. Lunenburg was reimbursed $37,233.82 by DVS, which is typically 75% of
  19|             what was given as assistance, the maximum allowable share. There are a couple of services that the Town is
  20|             reimbursed 100%.
  21|             Some examples of Veterans' Service Office assistance services include:
  22|             • Enrollment in chapter 115, State Veterans benefits
  23|             • Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  24|             • Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  25|             • Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes
  26|             • Payments of "Welcome Home" bonuses for deployed Veterans
  27|             • Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  28|             • Employment assistance
  29|                                                                                               69

===PAGE 70=== (ocr, 15 lines)
   1|                                                                            VETERANS' SERVICES
   2|             The Veterans' Service Office also provides information, advice, and assistance to any citizen interested in
   3|             learning about their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
   4|             • VA disability payments and property tax exemptions
   5|             • Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are declared 100% disa-
   6|                   bled from service-connected conditions.
   7|             • VA pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance, and death benefits
   8|             • VA medical care for both physical and mental needs
   9|                Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
  10|                   VA loans
  11|                   Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veterans or Civilian cemeteries)

  29|                                                       NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
  30|             Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the Lunenburg Board of Health in meeting the challenges of envi-
  31|             ronmental and public health needs in your community.
  32|             In 2024, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health (NABH) continued to advance regional public health through the ongoing
  33|             support of two key grants: the Public Health Excellence (PHE) Grant and the Contact Tracing and Case Investigation (CT/
  34|             CI) Grant.
  35|             Since 2022, the PHE Grant has enabled the onboarding of staff over time, including two Health Agents, an Education
  36|             Communication Specialist, and part- time staff to help with food inspections and manage the grant. This growing team has
  37|             enhanced NABH's ability to provide inspections, community outreach, and health education, fostering a more robust and
  38|             adaptable public health infrastructure across participating municipalities.
  39|             The CT/CI Grant has further supported NABH by funding a full-time Public Health Nurse, enabling enhancements to the
  40|             regional public health database, and incorporating advanced analytical tools to improve data tracking and reporting.
  41|             Together, these grants have strengthened NABH's ability to meet evolving community needs, improving disease surveil-
  42|             lance, emergency preparedness, and equitable access to high-quality public health services. Through a collaborative and re-
  43|             source-sharing approach, NABH has built a resilient and modern public health system that continues to effectively serve all
  44|             member communities.

   1|             • Nashoba staff attend and regularly participate in collaborative meetings, trainings, and conferences to keep current

   6|             Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary
   7|             The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has continued to evolve since its inception in
   8|             September 2021 to best support the needs of the district. In the past year, the PHE/CS has been communicating timely
   9|             and relevant public health information to towns and stakeholders and providing educational opportunities across the Dis-
  10|             trict with a social determinants of health (SDO) lens. This work has included:
  11|             • Providing community educational opportunities to residents both independently and collaboratively with the PH
  12|             nursing team based on community need - Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, etc.
  13|             • Overseeing and administering a grant and donation-funded community grief support program
  14|             • Offering and instructing adult mental health first aid certification courses for municipal staff, community volunteers,
  15|             and other groups serving NABH region residents
  16|             • Providing education and advocacy related to harm reduction, treatment, and de-stigmatization of substance use disor-
  17|             der
  18|             • Organizing technical assistance and resource-sharing meetings for municipalities related to opioid abatement settle-
  19|             ment funds
  20|             • Collaborating with the new Shared Services Coordinator to build district understanding of the State's Blueprint for
  21|             Public Health Excellence, maximize resources and identify opportunities for capacity building
  22|             • Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as quarterly newslet-
  23|             ters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information, and events such as flu and rabies clin-
  24|             ics. Continuing to explore and evaluate communication methods to reach as many community members as possible.
  25|             • Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community events to rep-
  26|             resent NABH
  27|             • Finalizing an official agency "mission statement", approved by the NABH Executive Committee in 2024
  28|             • Serving as a resource for community members with public health related questions across a broad range of topics
  29|             • Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization.
  30|             • PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to focus efforts
  31|             and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps.
  32|             • Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request including print,
  33|             electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials
  34|             • Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep information relevant and accessible
  35|             • Maintaining agency social media to reach more people in district with current, relevant, and seasonal public health
  36|             information
  37|             • Co-organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a springtime public health conference called
  38|             "Bridges to Health: Building Thriving Community Partnerships"
  39|                                                                                               75

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|             The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the
   3|             highest quality, most cost-effective fire, and emergency medical services to the citizens of the Town of Lunenburg.
   4|             In 2024, your Fire Department responded to 2083 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   5|             • Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions 447
   6|                   Emergency Medical 1193
   7|                  Inspections 414 Other 29
   8|             • This level of activity was equal to 2023.
   9|             The Department consists of 10 Career and 30-35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians,
  10|              lus a full-time Administrative Assistant. The Station is always staffed by three Firefighter/EMT's or Paramedics. Thi
  11|              overage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters. Our one issue we are confrontin
  12|             is having enough Per diem staff to cover the 96 hours of Per Diem coverage each week. This is particularly true of hav-
  13|             ing enough Part time Firefighter/Paramedics. Consideration needs to be made for an additional three Full Time staff to
  14|             allow us to cover 7 days/week with full time staff, not six as we do now. The Per diem coverage could then be reallocat-
  15|             ed to assist with staffing our second ambulance much of the day. A concern that is coming to the forefront with increas-
  16|             ing numbers of ambulance calls and longer transport and wait times at hospitals.
  17|             Staffing the Station with dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency
  18|             occurs. As many calls require more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the dedication and com-
  19|             mitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians.
  20|             The Department's Paramedic Program is now in its second year, and we began providing the service 24/7 on April 1,
  21|             2024. This is provided by six Full Time and seven On Call/Per diem Firefighter/Paramedics. Paramedic training is more
  22|             than two thousand hours and requires anywhere from 9-18 months of college level classroom education, clinical time,
  23|             and field internships to obtain certification. Firefighter/Paramedics Andrew Bolton and Jenny Smith serve as our ALS
  24|             and EMS Coordinators.
  25|             The loss of Nashoba Valley Medical Center in September has placed a significant strain on the region's EMS system.
  26|             While the impact on our Department has not been as severe as other Departments in the region, we are still seeing an
  27|             effect. The closure has created longer wait or turnaround times at the remaining hospitals. We are also seeing an uptick
  28|             in mutual aid requests as other communities' ambulances are seeing much longer transport and turnaround times. This
  29|             translates into longer and more frequent callbacks to cover our Station.
  30|             Lieutenant Christos Lekaditis and Firefighter Carl Gamberdella make up the Department's Fire Prevention Division and
  31|             in 2024 conducted 414 Inspections. This is in addition to multiple fire drills and public education initiatives. By law, we
  32|             are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold. We are also required to
  33|             inspect all new oll burners and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor license also requires a yearly
  34|             inspection for fire satety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire
  35|             protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken dur-
  36|            ing 2024 include:
  37|             • Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Education in
  38|                  both the schools and with Senior Citizens. This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring fire
  39|                   safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Firefighter Olivia Hayes serves as our Coordinator for this pro-
  40|                   gram.
  41|                 Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of walkway sand
  42|                  for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In partnership with this pro-
  43|                   gram, Firefighter Olivia Hayes delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for Smoke
  44|                  and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Firefighter Hayes can install free detectors
  45|                  obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  46|                                                                                               76

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                                                              FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke detectors.
   3|                Fire Prevention has become increasingly complex as codes change and new technologies appear. To keep pace, both
   4|                Inspectors hold certifications with the State as Fire Prevention Officers- Level 1 or 2. In addition two other members
   5|                have also completed the Level one training and other staff have completed the Basic Level training.
   6|                Six Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive Teams. These
   7|               regional teams respond to high risk and specialized rescue situations, such as Confined Space, Rescue
   8|                from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery. In conjunction
   9|                with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.
  10|                Our Apparatus fleet is currently in particularly good condition, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all under 10
  11|               years old. Our Rescue Pumper, Squad 3, remains in good condition though it is nearing the end of its projected life span.
  12|                This unit is scheduled for Replacement in FY'27. This unit is a dual-purpose Pumper and Rescue Unit, carrying the Jaws
  13|                of Life, High Pressure Air Bags, and other specialized rescue tools.
  14|                The Ambulances are experiencing increased wear and tear as we see not only more calls for service but longer transport
  15|                times to more distant hospitals and critical care facilities. 30 years ago, we had four hospitals within a 10-15-minute
  16|                transport time. Today there is only one. In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients require definitive treatment in
  17|               specialty facilities. This often requires transport to Worcester or occasionally even Boston. Given lengthy delays in ob-
  18|               taining new units, a replacement for Rescue 1, our 2014 ambulance was ordered in March of 2023. It is currently sched-
  19|                uled to be delivered in Mid-February 2025.
  20|                Generous support from the Town's share of American Rescue Plan funding allowed the Depart to obtain or replace some
  21|                much needed equipment, including:
  22|               • -The new Rescue 1 ambulance and related equipment (Mentioned above)
  23|               • -21 heavy duty portable radios
  24|               • -2 Ambulance grade Intravenous Fluid pumps (Needed for critical care transports)
  25|               • -Heavy Duty ambulance stretcher and auto-loading system (Retrofit to Rescue 2; Matches system in new Ambu-
  26|                     lance; significantly decreases the chances of back or other injuries to crews)
  27|               • -Powered stairchair for ambulance (For Patient extrication)
  28|               • -Replacement of several tables and chairs in the Fire Station
  29|               • State and Federal Grants provided additional equipment to the Department including:
  30|                           • -Training aids to teach "Stop the Bleed" classes.
  31|                           • -Two new Power Ventilation Saws
  32|                           • 3 Heavy Duty Portable Radios
  33|                           • Extrication Device for rescuing injured Firefighters from fires.
  34|                In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding commitment to
  35|                the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication are what truly makes the Department the high quality or-
  36|                ganization that it is. I do not say it enough, but so many of our staff deserve accolades for the work they do "behind the
  37|                scenes" every day to make the Department what it is. I cannot ask for a better group of men and women.
  38|                I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards, and commissions for their continuing help. The level of
  39|                inter-departmental cooperation that occurs daily is invisible to most people, but it is critical to the smooth operation of
  40|               the Town. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety. Such
  41|                support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to expect from us.
  42|                                                                                               77

   6|                                                                     2024 Police Department Town Report
   7|                      Mission Statement:
   8|                      We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services to
   9|                      our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that are
  10|                      realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed with our
  11|                      community and continuously look for ways to improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver police
  12|                      services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all individual
  13|                      rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend criminal offenders,
  14|                      and help resolve quality of life issues.
  15|                      Values:
  16|                           • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.
  17|                           • We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the
  18|                                community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department.
  19|                                We are a proactive problem-solving agency.
  20|                           • We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy.
  21|                                We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community.
  22|                           • We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource. We will
  23|                                strive to provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to
  24|                                enhance their professionalism.
  25|                           • We recognize that the wellbeing of our employees is of the upmost importance. We will strive to
  26|                                provide the necessary assistance to assure our employees enjoy a long and satisfying career.

  28|                       Department Review:
  29|                       It is an honor to provide the Lunenburg Police Department's Annual report as the Interim Chief of Police. In
  30|                       2024, the Department continued to face challenges associated with the economy, population growth, and
  31|                       increased traffic within the community. A nationwide trend of recruiting and retaining Police Officers continue
  32|                       to have an effect on our department, but it appears that we have been making progress in this area. Although
  33|                       faced with these challenges, the Department remains committed to being proactive and strives to make the
  34|                       Community safe for all.
  35|                       Chief Gammel retired in October after over 24 years of service to the Town of Lunenburg. We wish Chief
  36|                       Gammel the best of luck, good health, and a long and prosperous retirement.

===PAGE 84=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|              Use of Force:
   2|              The Department conducts an annual analysis of Use of Force incidents, as well as review(s) of the incidents as
   3|              they occur. In 2024, officers were involved in six (6) use of force incidents, which is a reduction in incidents
   4|              compared to eight (8) incidents in 2023. In each Use of Force incident de-escalation tactics, including the use
   5|              of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use of Force application. Officers continue to strive to de-
   6|              escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of Force.
   7|              One (1) officer was injured during the Use of Force. There were two (2) minor injuries reported by the
   8|              individuals during the application of Use of Force.
   9|              Motor Vehicle Pursuits:
  10|              The Department conducts an annual analysis of Motor Vehicle Pursuits, as well as review(s) of pursuits as they
  11|              occur. In 2024, officers were involved in one (1) motor vehicle pursuit, which is a reduction in incidents as
  12|              compared to three (3) 2023. There were no accidents, and no injuries to the public, officers or suspects(s)
  13|              involved in the pursuits that were reported.
  14|              Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District:
  15|              The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center
  16|              located in Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 911 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police,
  17|              Fire and EMS for the communities of Lunenburg, Berlin, Bolton, Boxborough, Havard, Lancaster, Devens, and
  18|              Department of Defense at Devens. NVRDD serves a total population of just over 35,000 residents and
  19|              thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms, businesses, and attractions within its six communities.

  14|               In 2023, NVRDD handled over 76,982 phone calls coming in on 911 and business lines. NVRDD also
  15|               coordinated the response to over 81,121 calls for service. NVRDD's staff are highly skilled in call-handling and
  16|               dispatching and are all able to perform both functions simultaneously. These first of the first responders provide
  17|               a vital and seamless link between the community and their local police, fire, and emergency medical services.
  18|               While the focus is on handling emergencies, their staff also serves the public and our responders by providing
  19|               other administrative and non-emergency dispatch tasks. NVRDD staffs the communications center with
  20|               multiple dispatchers and supervisors each shift to handle calls and requests.

  23|               community, we would not be successful. We look forward to the New Year and continue to bring the very best
  24|               in policing to our community.
  25|               The Department would like to wish retired Chief Thomas Gammel the best of luck in his retirement. We would
  26|               also like to thank him for his service to the Lunenburg Police Department and our Community. We wish you
  27|               the best of luck, and to a long, healthy and happy retirement.
  28|               Most importantly I would like to thank all the employees and their families of the Lunenburg Police Department
  29|               for their dedication to the Community, and the work that they perform daily. When working for a public service
  30|               department the employees work twenty-four hours a day 365 days a year, this is not an easy task for any Law
  31|               Enforcement Officer and their families. The personnel of the Lunenburg Department play a vital role in making
  32|               Lunenburg a great and safe place to live, work and visit for all.

===PAGE 88=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                            PLANNING BOARD
   2|             The Planning Board consists of 5 members elected for terms of 5 years each. It makes careful studies of the resources,
   3|             possibilities, and needs of the Town and plans for, and reviews, a variety of proposed short and long range development.
   4|             Included in this is making and maintaining a comprehensive, land use related Master Plan, setting forth in graphic and
   5|             textual form policies to govern the future growth and development of the entire Town, and that effort continued in FY24.
   6|             Related to that, the Board also oversees the statutory power to regulate the subdivision of land and the creation of public
   7|             ways within the Town by the adoption and administration of rules and regulations governing such development. More
   8|             broadly, the Board makes recommendations to Town Meeting on all matters affecting land use and growth, including the
   9|             Zoning By-Laws of the Town. Under its statutory and local permitting authority, it reviewed a variety of applications for
  10|             development and initiatives for regulatory amendments over 23 meetings in FY24, as outlined below. All applications to
  11|             the board are supported by a fee structure and use of peer review services paid for by the applicants, to offset administra-
  12|             tive costs to the Town.
  13|             SUBDIVISIONS
  14|             In FY24, under its Subdivision Rules and Regulations, the board held one public hearing to amend the application dead-
  15|             lines. Submitted applications included Subdivision Approval Not Required (ANR) plans, which are allowed by the Sub-
  16|             division Control Act and are an important part of the board's regulatory authority over divisions of land. Eleven (11)
  17|             ANR Plans not requiring approval under the Lunenburg Subdivision Rules and Regulations, but still requiring Board
  18|             endorsement, were approved in FY24. One (1) Definitive Plan application was also reviewed and approved. Under relat-
  19|             ed Zoning Bylaws Regulations, 1 Common Driveways application was approved.
  20|             STORMWATER MANAGEMENT
  21|             Lunenburg's Stormwater Bylaw requires Board review and approval for any development/redevelopment project that
  22|             disturbs 1 acre or more of land. This Bylaw safeguards public health, safety, welfare and the environment by minimizing
  23|             adverse impacts of contaminated stormwater both off-site and downstream. These requirements are more likely to affect
  24|             large commercial projects and multi-family dwellings, rather than average homeowner wanting to put in a shed, garden,
  25|             pool, etc. In FY24, the Board approved 5 stormwater permits.
  26|             SITE PLAN REVIEW
  27|             An application for construction of a 367,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building at 260 Falulah Road was
  28|             approved in FY23, and the site was being prepared for construction in FY24. The majority of the building will be located
  29|             on Airport Road/Crawford Street in Fitchburg, but a portion will extend into Lunenburg. In FY24, construction of a
  30|             372,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building approved in FY23 off Leominster-Shirley Road near the Shirley
  31|             town line was completed. As a result of this approval, improvements that were made to Leominster-Shirley Road with
  32|             the addition of an eastbound left-turn lane into the project and the widening of the road to the north (towards Reservoir
  33|             Road) on the project side of the street continued to improve safety, access and circulation. The Board also received and
  34|             approved 2 Site Plan Review applications in FY24, and several modifications to previously approved projects were con-
  35|             sidered and acted upon.
  36|             SCENIC ROADS
  37|             Lunenburg has 4 designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road, and Leominster Road.
  38|             Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as some of those mentioned in the paragraph above, being
  39|             created on some of these roads. As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance, reconstruction, or paving work in
  40|             the Town's right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls
  41|             without consent of the Planning Board. In FY24, 1 application was reviewed and approved by the Board.
  42|             ZONING
  43|             In FY24, the Board considered a variety of amendments to the Town's Zoning Bylaws. For Fall 23 Special Town Meet-
  44|             ing the articles included adjustments to Definitions; Use Regulations; Commercial and Office Park/Industrial
  45|                                                                                               88

===PAGE 89=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                             PLANNING BOARD
   2|                District Accessory Uses; Water Supply Protection; Solar Energy Systems; Dimensional Regula-
   3|                tions; General Provisions; and Driveways and Entrances, as well as changes to the Zoning Map. A
   4|                total of 10 amendments were proposed to Town Meeting. At the Annual Town Meeting in 2024, 3
   5|                additional amendments were proposed, which involved Energy Storage Systems, Scenic Roads and
   6|                Water Supply Protection once again. All but the Water Supply Protection articles passed.
   7|                In FY24, the Board also began to look at the MBTA Zoning requirements for compliance with State
   8|                statute by December 2025.
   9|                COMPREHENSIVE PLANNING
  10|               In FY24, the Planning Board continued supervising the update of the 2002 Master Plan. The con-
  11|                sultants for master planning services, OverUnder, RKG Associates Inc. and Innes Associates, held
  12|                public engagement meetings through June 2024. This early effort focused on public input to reflect
  13|                the vision, values, and goals of the community. Along with public meetings, public surveys that so-
  14|                licited input from Lunenburg residents on how they would like to influence the development of their
  15|                Town in the years to come, were developed and implemented.
  16|                Running alongside, the Master Plan Steering Committee, consisting of 15 voting members under
  17|                Charter with the Planning Board, also began and continued their role in facilitating public engage-
  18|                ment for the project. The committee's primary mission was fostering openness and inclusion in the
  19|                master planning process through dialogue and communication with the consulting team and various
  20|                stakeholders in the Lunenburg community. Through their work various essential land use boards,
  21|                committees and commissions were represented. In FY24, they held a total of 10 meetings to support
  22|                this effort.
  23|                While covering all of the above and more regularly scheduled meetings of the board continued at
  24|                Town Hall with a hybrid model; both in-person and via Zoom. As always, in FY24, the Board con-
  25|                tinued to look for ways to improve transparency and involvement.
  26|                The Planning Office that supports the Planning Board is located in the Ritter Memorial Building,
  27|                960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be reached at 978-582-4146 extension 5. Various forms are
  28|                available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/ on the Planning Board's homepage. The Zoning Map,
  29|                Code of the Town of Lunenburg, MA (which contains the Protective [Zoning] Bylaws), Rules and
  30|                Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval &
  31|                Special Permits, and Permitting Guidebook are available online.

   1|                                                                          ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|             The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2024. There were several applicants seeking
   3|             special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and
   4|             fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM on an "as needed" basis or Zoom as an alternative.
   5|             The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-Chairman,
   6|             Members: Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro and Donald Gurney, Associate mem-
   7|             bers. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
   8|              The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
   9|              1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  10|              2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  11|             3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  12|                   amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from
  13|                   the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  14|                   Administrative Official.
  15|                 To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  16|                   40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under
  17|                   Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local
  18|                   zoning ordinances.
  19|              The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue,
  20|             Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  21|              Thursday, closed Friday. If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-
  22|              4146, ext 421 during regular business hours.Zoning Board applications are available in the ottice and online at
  23|              www.lunenburgma.gov

   2|              The Open Space Committee's efforts in FY24 principally focused on gaining a better understanding
   3|              of the Town of Lunenburg's portfolio of vacant, undeveloped, "general municipal" lands, and as-
   4|              sessing which of those properties may be repurposed more specifically to meet needs identified in
   5|              the Town's Open Space and Recreation Plan. The Open Space Committee began discussions of the
   6|              required 2027 update to the Open Space Plan.

   1|                                                                    LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
   2|                                                                                 Town Report 2024
   3|                                                                            Superintendent's Message
   4|             I am grateful for the seven years I have been able to lead and serve the students, staff and families of the Lunenburg
   5|             Public Schools. I am very fortunate to have worked in partnership with the Lunenburg School Committee which has
   6|             consistently held serving the best interests of the students as their main focus. I am appreciative for the support of the
   7|             Town Manager, Select Board and Finance Committee as we have worked to meet the educational needs of the children
   8|             of Lunenburg.
   9|             The district has remained focused on execution of the Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our
  10|             Mission and Vision: The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence,
  11|             and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and re-
  12|             warding lives.
  13|             The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of attending to
  14|             the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered instruction for deeper learn-
  15|             ing at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all.
  16|             The Lunenburg School Committee approved the Strategy for Continuous Improvement in November 2019 and since
  17|             then we have worked with intent to achieve our vision by putting the necessary infrastructure in place to support the well
  18|             -being and academic achievement of all students.
  19|             Over the last six years, we adhered to our Strategy action plans, even through the pandemic. At one time the Strategy
  20|             had four overarching objectives and eleven action plans. We worked in a focused way each year until the Strategy was
  21|             reduced to one overarching objective and three action plans last year. During the 2024-2025 school year, we have con-
  22|             tinued working on those plans. One of the action plans focused on updating our core values to reflect the culture and
  23|             commitment of our district. In the fall of 2024, guided by Dr. Kalise Wornum, the Leadership Team began this initial work.
  24|             Guided by the Strategy for Continuous Improvement since November 2019 and aided by federal and state grant funding,
  25|             over the last six years the district was able to:
  26|             • Add a curriculum leadership and instructional coaching
  27|             • Increase district behavioral health staffing
  28|                   Adopt a standards-aligned, rigorous on-grade-level curriculum in ELA/Literacy K-8, math K-8
  29|             • Adopt a districtwide Tier 1 SEL curriculum
  30|                  Implement district benchmark assessments in literacy and math
  31|             •     Use assessment data in order to determine need for intervention
  32|               Create an intervention model for academics and behavioral health
  33|                  Create a referral process for intervention
  34|                 Increase common planning time
  35|             • Maintain class sizes under twenty-five
  36|             • Create and execute a communication plan using consistent platforms districtwide
  37|             As a result, we have seen improvement in student growth and achievement. We must stay the course in order to see more
  38|             progress and improvement.
  39|             The district has continued working to build a more inclusive culture in our schools. In the fall of 2023, the district estab-
  40|             lished a Culture & Climate Advisory Council comprised of parents/guardians, staff and high school student representa-
  41|             tives. This Council will help the district to foster increased family engagement in the education of their student. In June
  42|             2024, the Council presented the School Committee with a Family-School Compact, an overarching document that articu-
  43|             lates the roles of the student, parent/guardian, and educator in the education of each student. During the 2024-2025
  44|             school year, the Council will continue working with each school to create a document that "translates" the overarching
  45|             Compact into a school specific document which outlines what the Compact looks, feels and sounds like in each
  46|             school. The Council will also review and recommend an evidence based anti-bullying curriculum for School Committee
  47|             adoption, as well as conduct the biennial review of the district's Bullying Prevention and Intervention Plan.
  48|                                                                                               93

===PAGE 94=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                    SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
   2|             In the first half of FY24 as we began the FY25 budget cycle, we experienced significant challenges as the School De-
   3|             partment was without a School Business Manager and the Town Manger was without a Town Accountant/Director of
   4|             Finance and Assistant Town Accountant. This was further complicated by an upgrade to the Town's financial manage-
   5|             ment platform, MUNIS. Challenges continued with a widening gap between the available local budget funding and in-
   6|             creasing operational costs. Through the FY25 budget cycle the School Committee and school administration worked
   7|             with the Town Manager, Finance Committee and Select Board to find a way to fund a bare bones needs-based budg-
   8|             et. However, with the support of invested parents/guardians, members of the Select Board and Finance Committee, and
   9|             Lunenburg residents an override for the schools was passed. As a result, the district only lost ten positions due to the sun
  10|             setting of COVID era grants. While we are extremely grateful for the override, the override did not provide a longer
  11|             term solution to the fundamental issue; incoming revenues are not keeping pace with operational costs. Therefore, the
  12|             Select Board created a Budget Task Force with a representative from the School Committee, Finance Committee, Select
  13|             Board, as well as the Town Manager, Superintendent, and Finance Directors from the school department and the
  14|             town. The Task Force worked July through December 2024 to review the town's financial situation and bring forth rec-
  15|             ommendations to improve the town's financial outlook.                        While recommendations were brought forward and may result
  16|             in some additional revenue, there were no recommendations that would vastly change available revenue for operational
  17|             budgets moving forward.
  18|             The School Committee and school administration has also maintained a focus on the aging Turkey Hill Elementary
  19|             School. In FY2, a study to assess the current condition of the building was funded. By April 2025, we will have a re-
  20|             port with projected costs for all projects required to bring the building into code and ADA compliance. The report will
  21|             be used to inform decisions regarding the future of the Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  22|             Over the last seven years, we have utilized all available resources and worked with intent to impact student achievement
  23|             and overall well-being in the years immediately following the COVID years and beyond. We have remained true to the
  24|             Strategy for Continuous Improvement and have seen improved outcomes over the last two years. However, if we expect
  25|             to see continued improvement in student achievement, proper staffing levels and curriculum resources are neces-
  26|             sary. This will be one of our greatest challenges in the next several years as grant funding sources have waned and re-
  27|             volving accounts are no longer sufficient to support operational costs in any meaningful way.
  28|             I will be retiring in June 2025 bringing a career as an educator spanning thirty-eight years to a close. I am grateful to
  29|             have been so lucky as to have the opportunity to lead the Lunenburg Public Schools. I want to express my heart-felt
  30|             gratitude to the Lunenburg School Committee for trusting me to take their priorities and hopes for the district and make
  31|             them a reality. I must also acknowledge and thank our dedicated faculty and staff for their professionalism, flexibility,
  32|             and hard work. Without the commitment and effort of every member of our staff we would not have seen more equita-
  33|             ble, meaningful, and impactful changes benefitting every student's learning experience in Lunenburg. Collectively we
  34|             have been dedicated to and focused on our overarching objectives in order to provide a quality education for the children
  35|             of Lunenburg. So much good work has been done, but there is much more to do. I trust the Lunenburg School Commit-
  36|             tee with find a new Superintendent who will be equally committed to the work.
  37|             Each year I conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent's Award. I am pleased to announce
  38|             Cashel Daly as the recipient of the 2025 Superintendent's Award. In addition to many academic achievements, includ-
  39|             ing National Honor Society membership, Cashel has enjoyed his time as a Scout, member of the LHS cross country
  40|             team, and as a volunteer for the Lunenburg Community Food Pantry and Bright Eyed, a local non-profit providing week-
  41|             end meal kits for school-age children dealing with food insecurity. Cashel intends to study history and hopes to be a his-
  42|             torian. He particularly enjoys study of the U.S. presidents. Not surprisingly his favorite quote is one from Theodore
  43|             Roosevelt, "It is hard to fail, but it is worse never to have tried to succeed."
  44|             Respectfully submitted,
  45|             Dr. Kate Burnham, Superintendent

  25|                                                                         School Facilities Department
  26|             Lunenburg has made significant progress on capital planning projects and preventative maintenance needs.
  27|             Capital Program
  28|             The following projects have been completed or are underway:
  29|             1. LMHS Turf Field Feasibility Study- Collins Engineering was contracted for ($50,000) to review the turf field area
  30|                  and recommended alternatives to improve drainage conditions. The middle/high school area near the turf, a concrete
  31|                   Bin Block retaining wall at the metal bleachers which supports the press box, a concrete masonry unit retaining wall,
  32|                   a paver block walkway, and the playing field and track area, as well as the immediate surrounding areas were con-
  33|                  sidered. Findings of this study recommended improvements to the concrete Bin Block, CMU retaining wall, and re-
  34|                   grading the lower grass open area with cost estimates at $920,000.
  35|             2. Lunenburg Primary School Mini-Split Project- Completed during the summer of 2024, allowing for 6 Condensing
  36|                   Units feeding 12 classrooms on the second floor to be installed at a cost of $232,800. At this point, these units pro-
  37|                   vide cooling in the warmer months in a more energy efficient way and reduce heat load/stressors on the building. In
  38|                  the fall of 2024, a LPS district team supported by Lunenburg Green Communities submitted a grant application to
  39|                  pursue additional funding to install the remaining LPS classrooms with mini-split units. Once both phases are com-
  40|                  pleted, units can be used to optimize heating and cooling throughout the building. These units are eligible for energy
  41|                  rebates from Unitil. Added electrical costs for operation will be offset to some degree by solar net metering credits.
  42|             3. Window Safety Film was applied to Turkey Hill Elementary School ($33,569) and the Lunenburg Middle High
  43|                   School ($33,250). Benefits of both projects 1) create a barrier that does not allow window glass to immediately shat-
  44|                   ter; 2) decrease solar glare into the rooms thus decreasing the heat load while decreasing cooling costs (where appli-
  45|                  cable).
  46|                                                                                               95

===PAGE 96=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|             4. Turkey Hill Study for Renovation and Assessments and Schematic Design ($275,000) is expected to be completed by
   2|             May 1, 2025. Findings and recommendations will be provided by CSS Architects in a thorough report with estimated
   3|             costs. Drawings, photos, schematics, and concept plans will be in the final report to explain and illustrate proposed reno-
   4|             vation /repair /reconstruction solutions that would allow Turkey Hill to meet MEP/FP and ADA requirements.
   5|             The Fire Alarm Upgrade Project ($322,240) is under contract with work planned for the summer of 2025.
   6|             5. The district has completed purchase of a Utility Pick-Up Truck with Plow ($64,780) through the McGovern Dealer-
   7|             ship in Marlborough which will replace an existing truck with extensive repair needs.
   8|             6. Upgrades for Security Systems (LPS/THES) and Exterior Doors (THES) have been placed out to bid, and the district
   9|             is evaluating next steps based on cost.
  10|             Preventative Maintenance
  11|             The custodial team is working diligently to attend to preventative maintenance needs. Some of our more time consuming
  12|             tasks have required significant attention to HVAC needs within each building that include regular inspections and allo-
  13|             cating funds for supplies as well as contracted services. Due to rising costs associated with transportation, material avail-
  14|             ability, and labor, our operations and maintenance budget is continuously challenged to meet facility needs.

  18|             Town Report 2024
  19|             Lunenburg Public Schools IT Review
  20|             The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department played a critical role in addressing the technol-
  21|             ogy needs both inside and outside of the classroom. The Department also focused heavily on compliance for E911 on the
  22|             phone systems. The following projects were completed as part of an emphasis on E911 compliance:
  23|             1. Re-cabling the Turkey Hill Elementary School. This upgrade from CAT5 cabling allowed an increase in the overall
  24|             network speeds as well as support for a phone system.
  25|             2. Installation of a new phone system and phones in every room at the Turkey Hill Elementary School. This helped get
  26|             Turkey Hill Elementary School in line with multiple E911 compliance laws by having phones which can dial out in eve-
  27|             ry instructional area.
  28|             3. Phone server CPU upgrades at both the Lunenburg Middle High School and the Lunenburg Primary School.
  29|             4. Switch to SIP Trunking phone plan which will finish the compliance in which every phone has the ability to call out
  30|             and the emergency personnel will receive both the building as well as the room number of the caller.
  31|             Solutions supporting cyber safety were also addressed this year. PhishID Phishing Protection was implemented which
  32|             scans and detects malicious login pages for staff. The district also implemented KnowB4 which sends out phishing simu-
  33|             lation emails as well as weekly and monthly cyber tips to all staff members to help protect against cyber criminals.
  34|             Technology continued to be a prevalent tool in the classroom. In response, the district continued to support devices for
  35|             each student by providing new devices to 6th and 9th graders as well as introducing new devices in grades k-5 to replace
  36|             end of life equipment.
  37|             The IT Department continued to manage and respond to all help desk requests with an extra focus on following best
  38|             practices, student technology usage and excellent customer service. The team will continue to direct efforts towards im-
