   2|                                              Town Report - FY24 (data taken from 2023-2024 school year)
   3|             From the March 1, 2024 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), there are 242 students with disabilities
   4|             receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 228). This represents 15.5% of our total student
   5|             population of 1,566 students (it was 14.6% last year). The state average from the 2023-2024 elected Populations Report (data was
   6|             last updated on Feb. 22, 2024) (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/selectedpopulations.aspx) is 20.2%. From the March 1,
   7|             2024 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), the total number of students receiving services outside
   8|             the district was 8 (last year was 7). Of the 8 students, 4 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 3 are in Day Placements and 1 are in
   9|             Residential Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves students ages 18-22 years old, had 7
  10|             students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the March 1, 2024 report, 119 students identified with a disability received accommo-
  11|             dations via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last year was 155).
  12|             For the 2024 school year, our Accountability Overall Classification was "not requiring assistance or intervention". The reason for
  13|             this classification was "Substantial Progress Towards Targets" at 60% (last year it was 57%). This data can be found at the DESE
  14|             http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&
  15|             Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the whole Special Services Depart-
  16|              ment. DESE requires us to collect data for certain Indicators they are targeting each year. For our Indicators, the following infor-
  17|              mation is available below and also can be found at: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&
  18|             Indicator 1 - Graduation Rate. The state target for Students with IEPs was 78.33% (was 77.33% last year). Lunenburg's rate was
  19|             61.9% (was 66.7% last year) and the overall state rate was 80.4% (was 82.1% last year) for Students with IEPs.
  20|             Indicator 2 - Dropout Rate. The state target for Students with IEPs was 12.97% (was 13.4% last year). Lunenburg rate for Students
  21|             with IEPs was "Suppressed" due to enrollment counts fewer than 6 (it was 11.1% last year) and the overall state rate was 13.6% (was
  22|             11.4% last year).
  23|             Indicator 3C - Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS-Alt. "In Massachusetts the highest possible score on the MCAS-
  24|             Alt, the state's alternate assessment, is Progressing. Therefore, the data reported in the SPP/APR for the "Number of Children with
  25|             IEPs Scoring At or Above Proficient Against Alternate Academic Achievement Standards is zero 'O'' District-level data on the per-
  26|             formance of all students with IEPs on MCAS-Alt is available at:
  27|             http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/achievement_alt_level.aspx?linkid=116&orgcode=01620000&fycode=2023&orgtypecode=5
  28|             Indicator 4 - Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs. For Lunenburg, our rate was 0.0% (last year it was
  29|             0.0%). The state rate was .1%. Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant discrepancies with the Suspension/Expulsion rates by
  30|             race or ethnicity. In 2022 (most current data available), Lunenburg was not found to have any significant discrepancies due to these
  31|             factors.
  32|             Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2022-2023, the state's rates are as follows: for Full
  33|             Inclusion of students = 67.2%, Partial Inclusion = 13.0%, Substantially Separate Placements = 13.2%, and for Separate Schools/
  34|             Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 6.6%. In Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows: Full Inclusion = 72.0%, Partial
  35|             = 11.7%, Substantially Separate = 14.0% and Separate schools, Residential, etc = 2.3%.
  36|             Indicator 6 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs. The 2022-202 state rates showed the percent of students
  37|             receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion early childhood program was 50.2%, in a Par-
  38|             tial Inclusion Program was 17.4%, a Substantially Separate Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facili-
  39|             ty) was 21.1%, and students not attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was
  40|             11.2%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 33.3%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 9.5%, Substantially Separate
  41|             rate was 57.1%, and Related Services was 0%.
  42|             Indicator 7 - Early Childhood Outcomes. Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4 year cycle in which they
  43|             report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may occur at any time during the subsequent three
  44|             year period as the children leave the preschool program. Lunenburg's 2022-2023 cohort reporting year results are suppressed for
  45|             districts reporting 10 or fewer students.

===PAGE 98=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|             Indicator 8 - Parent Involvement. This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special education who report that
   2|             school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results for students with IEPs. For 2022-2023 the state
   3|             target was 89.5%. Lunenburg's rate was 7.3% and the state's rate was 10.7% (from information collected in the Spring of 2022).
   4|             Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special Education Catego-
   5|              ries. In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either case.
   6|             Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent to evaluate, who were
   7|              evaluated within the State established timeline (2017-2018). Data is reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October, Novem-
   8|              ber, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated 90% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was
   9|             100%.
  10|             Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, found eligible for special
  11|              education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in
  12|              100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed" (last school year reviewed was 2017-18). Data is suppressed if the number of stu-
  13|              dents referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than 10.
  14|              Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%. This indicator measures the %
  15|              of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updat-
  16|              ed and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably ena-
  17|             ble the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg
  18|              was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.
  19|             Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of students with IEPs who exited high
  20|             school during the 2022-2023 school year and self-reported post-school engagement in education or employment one year after leav-
  21|             ing high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  22|              Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  23|             Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high school.
  24|              Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training program; or competitively em-
  25|             ployed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  26|              Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-secondary activity one
  27|             year after leaving high school. For the 2022-2023 school year, state targets for the three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are
  28|             Measurement A: 36.9%; Measurement B: 70.4%; and Measurement C: 77%. Lunenburg rates are as follows (from reporting year
  29|              2022-2023): Measurement A = 28.57%, Measurement B =57.14%, and Measurement C = 71.43%.

  33|             son. The executive board is made up of a Chairperson: Cheri Cordella.

===PAGE 99=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                                                     TEACHING & LEARNING MODELS AND PD UPDATE
   2|                                                                                 Town Report 2024
   3|             Teaching and learning is at the center of all of our work. This past year, we worked diligently to provide high-quality
   4|             curricular materials and instruction to students, provide strategic interventions and challenges to our learners, and to pro-
   5|             vide professional development opportunities for our educators to support their efforts.
   6|             Providing educators with high quality, evidence based, and inclusive curricular materials is a priority for the state of
   7|             Massachusetts' Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) and for our district. Access to these types
   8|             of curricular materials can "prompt improvement in student outcomes" as evidenced by research. To this end in 2023-
   9|             2024, educators:
  10|             → implemented the second year of our new English language arts and math curriculums, HMH Into Reading and Into
  11|                   Math, at grades K-5. HMH online professional development was provided on both the platform and content. Pro-
  12|                   fessional Learning Community (PLC) meetings occurred to review assessment data, make adjustments to instruc-
  13|                   tional practice based on the data, improve instruction through coaching and modeling, and module planning based on
  14|                   priority standards.
  15|             → began implementation of Amplify, our new middle school language arts curriculum. Educators taught all of the mod-
  16|                  ules at least once and made adjustments and refinements to practice. The specific focus in the first year was looking
  17|                  at each of the program components and determining which were best implemented through the online platform, and
  18|                  which were better to do on paper or through the use of Amplify's supplemental materials that supported the curricu-
  19|                  lum. Additional work was done on pacing to ensure that the critical units were taught and mastered.
  20|             → completed a process for selecting a new high-quality math program for our middle school as well as developed an
  21|                  implementation process. Middle school math teachers created the criteria by which they would select a new math
  22|                  program. Once that document was finalized, a curriculum review of all high-quality middle school math programs
  23|                   was completed in PLC meetings. Three programs were selected for a pilot of one month each, using all components
  24|                   of the program's units. Grade levels documented their feedback and a group meeting was held to discuss the merits
  25|                   of each program. It was unanimously decided to choose the EdGems program based on its quality, and combination
  26|                   of differentiated online and paper content that was easily accessible to teachers and students.
  27|             MTSS
  28|             A multi-tiered system of support (MTSS) was implemented at Lunenburg Primary and Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  29|              The key components of MISS include universal screening and progress monitoring tools: a process to analyze data, plan
  30|             for interventions, and monitor student progress; a tiered system of support where every student receives core instruction
  31|             and some students receive strategic or intensive instruction; and a collaborative team-based approach. In short, MTSS is
  32|             a way of doing things within the schools that impacts every student. At each school, teams of educators came together to
  33|             plan for and check student growth. They worked with our literacy and math specialists to analyze multiple data points so
  34|             that strategic and intensive interventions for support and/or enrichment were provided. Student progress was monitored
  35|             and adjustments were made as necessary.
  36|             Lunenburg Public Schools utilizes multiple assessments to evaluate student achievement and growth. Students in grades
  37|             K-8 completed a fall, winter and spring iReady assessment in both reading and mathematics and the personalized learn-
  38|             reading and mathematics. The Dynamic Indicators of Basic Literacy Skills (DIBELS) is used in grades K-3 to deter-
  39|             mine how students are performing on the critical skills necessary for reading: phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency,
  40|             vocabulary, and comprehension. In mathematics grades K-5, the Forefront Universal Screener for Number Sense was
  41|             used to determine students' early numeracy competency.
  42|             Structured common planning time for teachers in grade level continued for grades K-8. Our district literacy coaches met
  43|             with teachers during common planning time to support curriculum development and help foster student growth. High
  44|             school department heads structured department meeting time to facilitate the necessary curriculum work needed to move
  45|             each department forward and improve learning for students.
  46|             PD Focus
  47|             For professional development, we worked with Dr. Kalise Wornum, owner of KW diversity. Dr. Wornam provided dis-
  48|             trict staff with multiple sessions on cultural competence; we received positive feedback around the impact of this work
  49|             on educators' planning and instruction, and plan to have Dr. Wornum return next year to focus more specifically on Cul-
  50|             turally Responsive Practices: Teacher Moves.
  51|                                                                                               99

   5|               School District participated in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Literacy Academy, the Inclusive Practices Academy

  20|                  Lunenburg Public Schools food service participates in the National School Lunch Program and the School Breakfast Program.
  21|                  As part of this program, our schools offer healthy meals every school day. The Commonwealth is supplementing National
  22|                  School Lunch Program funds to ensure all students have access to healthy school meals in Massachusetts, regardless of house-
  23|                  hold income. Universal free meal has led to the growth in participation in our program. Students are able to participate without
  24|                  the worry of overtly being identified as low income.

  26|                  uses LINQ Connect for Point of sale including student and staff account payment/management.

  28|                  produced food each month in our cafeterias. We collaborated with Fairview Orchards a small family farm located in Groton,
  29|                  Massachusetts. A third generation run business, with a mission to bring local fresh fruit to schools in their community and sur-
  30|                  rounding areas. The students and staff have enjoyed the delicious apples from Fairview Orchids. We hope to continue this part-
  31|                  nership with Fairview for many years.

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 30 lines)
   1|                                                                    LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                Lunenburg Primary School is a student-focused early childhood learning school dedicated to serving children from
   3|                 preschool through grade 2. With students ranging in age from three to eight years old, we are committed to fostering a
   4|                 nurturing and inclusive environment where each child's academic, social, and emotional growth is supported.
   5|                Our school community consists of students, families, teachers, instructional staff, and support personnel who
   6|                collaborate to ensure that every child's individual needs are met. By focusing on the whole development of each
   7|                student, we strive to cultivate not only strong academic skills but also essential social and emotional competencies that
   8|                will serve them throughout their educational journey and beyond. At Lunenburg Primary School, we continue to create
   9|                a welcoming, enriching atmosphere where all children feel valued and encouraged to reach their full potential. We
  10|                look forward to another year of growth, learning, and community together.
  11|                 As of October 1, 2024, Lunenburg Primary School reported a total student enrollment of 382 students to the
  12|                Department of Elementary and Secondary Education. This includes 49 preschool students who attend various half-day
  13|                 sessions, across two classrooms. The school also serves Kindergarten through second grade, with the following
  14|                enrollment breakdown:
  15|                • Kindergarten: 110 students distributed across five classrooms, with class sizes ranging from 21 to 23 students per
  16|                • Grade 1: 109 students, also across five classrooms, with class sizes ranging from 18 to 22 students.
  17|                 • Grade 2: 114 students, distributed across five classrooms, with class sizes ranging from 22 to 25 students.
  18|                 This structure ensures that each grade level is supported by dedicated classroom spaces, allowing for an optimal
  19|                learning environment for our students. We are proud to provide a strong foundation for academic growth and
  20|                 development at Lunenburg Primary School.
  21|                 At Lunenburg Primary School, our professional development efforts have been focused on enhancing inclusive
  22|                practices and refining our instructional strategies. Central to this is our continued use of the HMH Into Reading and
  23|                Into Math curricula, which guide our approach to literacy and mathematics instruction. Common Planning Time
  24|                 remains a priority in our building schedule, enabling teachers to collaborate, analyze student performance data, and
  25|                 align instructional practices to meet student needs effectively. We have also leveraged consultants and district
  26|                 curriculum coordinators to support our teaching staff in developing evidence-based instruction and fostering a
  27|                 consistent learning experience across grade levels. In addition to academic growth, we place a strong emphasis on
  28|                cultivating a positive, inclusive, and supportive school culture. Our staff consistently works to create an environment
  29|                where all students feel valued and empowered.
  30|                                                                                              102

===PAGE 103=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|             We continue to implement the Responsive Classroom model in every classroom to foster a sense of community and pro-
   2|             mote social-emotional learning. Furthermore, we have continued with our use of Positive Behavioral Interventions and
   3|             Supports (PBIS), a research-backed, tiered framework designed to improve student outcomes by integrating data, sys-
   4|             tems, and practices that positively impact student behavior and engagement.
   5|             One of the highlights of our school culture is the monthly themes we introduce for both students and staff to explore to-
   6|             gether. These themes serve as a foundation for discussions and activities that promote growth and reflection. Additional-
   7|             ly, through our collaboration with our School Resource Officer (SRO) and his emotional support canine, Hank, we con-
   8|             tinue with the Hank's Helper awards. These awards recognize students who consistently demonstrate kindness, respect,
   9|             and helpfulness to others in the school community. During our monthly assemblies, students are also selected to have
  10|             "Snack with the Principal," a special opportunity for small groups of students to share their experiences and connect with
  11|             the leadership team in a more personal setting. Through these initiatives, we are dedicated to fostering an environment
  12|             that supports both academic achievement and the development of character, ensuring that all students feel supported,
  13|             recognized, and valued in their educational journey.
  14|             Lunenburg Primary School is a Title One school, and we are committed to providing all students with the support they
  15|             need to succeed. To ensure that every child has access to the services and resources they require, we have continued to
  16|             refine our tiered instructional system. As part of this system, we conduct common assessments in the fall, winter, and
  17|             spring for all students in grades K-2 in the areas of reading and math. These assessments help us monitor the effective-
  18|             ness of our instruction and track the progress of each student. By using data from these assessments, we are able to iden-
  19|             tify struggling students early and provide targeted interventions through our Multi-Tiered Support System (MTSS).
  20|             Our MTSS approach ensures that students receive the right level of support based on their individual needs. This in-
  21|             cludes specialized interventions in reading, math, speech, and occupational therapy. Additionally, the MTSS model ex-
  22|             tends to social-emotional support for students who may need assistance in managing their emotions or behaviors. For
  23|             these students, we work closely with our Behavioral Health Support Team, which offers targeted interventions and strat-
  24|             egies to help them succeed both academically and socially. By using a comprehensive and data-driven approach, we aim
  25|             to ensure that all students at Lunenburg Primary School are equipped with the tools and support necessary to thrive in
  26|             their educational journey.
  27|             The Lunenburg Primary School Council meets monthly to address school-related concerns and provide guidance to the
  28|             principal. As an advisory group, the council plays a key role in reviewing the school building budget and contributing to
  29|             the development of the school improvement plan.
  30|             The members of the Council for the 2024-2025 school year are as follows:
  31|             • Principal: Carla Squier
  32|             • Assistant Principal: Michael Smith
  33|             • Teachers: Kirsten Snape, Nicole Bienvenu,
  34|             • Parents: Gretchen Moscon, Janet Winer
  35|             • School Committee Representative: Brian Lehtinen
  36|             This diverse group of individuals brings a variety of perspectives and expertise to the table, ensuring that decisions are
  37|             made in the best interest of the school community and its students. The Primary School Council continues to collaborate
  38|             in support of the school's mission to provide a positive and effective learning environment for all students.
  39|             We are truly grateful for the opportunity to work alongside our highly dedicated district-wide colleagues and a caring
  40|             community to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our youngest students. The support and collabora-
  41|             tion we receive from our families, staff, and community partners continue to make a meaningful difference in the lives of
  42|             our students. For more information about the work we do at Lunenburg Primary School, please visit our website: https://
  43|             sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/
  44|             Respectfully submitted,
  45|             Carla Squier, Principal

===PAGE 106=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                             TURKEY HILL ELEMENTARY SCHOOL
   2|             Turkey Hill Elementary School is an elementary school serving students in third grade through fifth grade. In the fall of
   3|              2024, Turkey Hill Elementary School welcomed 345 enrolled students. There are five sections of grade 3, grade 4, and
   4|              grade 5. Grade 3 has 125 students. Grade 4 has 106 students and Grade 5 has 114 students.
   5|             Our unified arts areas include physical education, art, music, library, and STEM. We are also served by a full time assis-
   6|             tant principal, full time nurse, a psychologist, a full time adjustment counselor/social worker and a guidance counse-
   7|             lor, and 21 full time teachers. Other support personnel who work across the district include an ELA coach, a Math
   8|             coach, a director of teaching and learning, two EL teachers, a speech and language pathologist, BCBA, OT, and PT. In
   9|              addition, we have one interventionist tutors providing targeted support for small groups of students.
  10|             Staff continually work on the development of the whole child by supporting students' development and growth in the
  11|              areas of social emotional learning, literacy, math, social studies and science. We are able to meet student needs with the
  12|              support of the district curriculum of teaching and learning and the two coaches which enables teachers to have the op-
  13|              portunity to meet at grade levels to develop and provide evidence based instruction, common learning experiences, stu-
  14|              dent data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support student academic growth. Students continue to have
  15|             benchmark assessments three times throughout the school year (beginning, middle and end of year that are used to help
  16|             us celebrate students' academic growth and effort. The data provided by these assessments also helps refine our focus
  17|              for interventions.
  18|             This fall our fifth grade students had their annual trip to Nature's classroom. It was a successful week with good weather,
  19|              community building, and the creation of fond memories of a unique time together, thanks to the combined efforts of our
  20|             entire fifth grade staff.
  21|              We held an in-person open house in September. It was a well-attended event and for many parents their first opportunity
  22|             to be physically present in our building. It was accompanied by a book fair facilitated by our librarian, Mrs. Macnama-
  23|             ra. Parent conferences in January offered both in person and virtual options and appointments were fully booked within
  24|              a few days.
  25|              We held our annual Penny Wars fundraiser, it was a huge success and the money raised will be put toward the purchase
  26|              and installation of a shared structure in our playing field. As always, our PTO assisted in the weeklong collection. We
  27|             could not be successful without their efforts. We were able to bring back a fifth grade band and all students will be per-
  28|              forming in an upcoming spring concert under the direction of our music teacher, Mr. Anthony Balester. The courtyard/
  29|              outdoor learning space was further refined with new stone and handicap access thanks to community and PTO dona-
  30|             tions. Turkey Hill also held its annual Turkey Trot in November. Led by Mr. S. Ludtke, the turkey trot was a wonderful
  31|              experience.
  32|              The Turkey Hill School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns. Serving as an advisory group to
  33|             the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and developing the school improvement
  34|             Members of the Council for the 2023-2024 school year are:
  35|              Principal: Norman Yvon
  36|              Assistant Principal: Bethann Cormier
  37|             Teachers: Kate Robinson, Rhonda Connery
  38|             Parents: Matthew Brenner, Laura Brothers, Elizabeth Noyes
  39|             School Committee Rep: Laura Brzozoski
  40|              Our school's success relies upon the combined and collaborative efforts of our wonderful staff, parents, and community
  41|              members. We continue to seek out opportunities to engage and effectively communicate with our families. . I am hon-
  42|              ored to be chosen as your principal.
  43|             For more information about the work that we do at the Turkey Hill Elementary School please visit our website: https://
  44|             sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/thms/welcome
  45|             Respectfully submitted,
  46|             Norman P. Yvon, Principal

===PAGE 109=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                    LUNENBURG MIDDLE SCHOOL
   2|             Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support students'
   3|             social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, creative, lifelong learner
   4|             in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication. The dedicated and ac-
   5|             complished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child academically,
   6|             socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community members that
   7|             possess the skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   8|             Lunenburg Middle School is a co-located middle - high school serving grades six through eight. Our effective, student
   9|             centered middle school embraces interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with smaller groups of
  10|             students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their preferred learning style, making
  11|             them more confident and successful.
  12|             Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of different inter-
  13|             ests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. One of our goals is for stu-
  14|             dents to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they move through high school
  15|             and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster critical thinking and problem solving
  16|             skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or where their passion may lead them to.
  17|             Our grade eight team of teachers continue to implement a schedule that mirrors the high school schedule. Our philoso-
  18|             phy is to ease the difficult transition from middle school to the first year of high school by developing student's inde-
  19|             pendent skills they will require to be successful in high school. Grade eight teachers function as one inclusive team
  20|             where students take classes with a variety of different teachers as they would in a high school setting. This differs from
  21|             our two interdisciplinary team of teachers that have been a successful model in grades six and seven.
  22|             LMS continues to utilize the i-Ready diagnostic to provide an additional tool to assess students' skills in addition to
  23|             classroom instruction. Parents will be provided fall, winter and spring benchmark assessment reports created by i- ready
  24|             so they too can monitor student progress.
  25|             Lunenburg Middle School has benefited from a top notch guidance support team. A guidance counselor and a school
  26|             social worker coupled with our shared school psychologist has been able to provide a high level of social emotional sup-
  27|             port to our students and their families. Our guidance team has been able to meet the needs of our students while prepar-
  28|             ing them to be available for academic learning.

   1|                                                                                 Athletics and Clubs
   2|             LMS successfully participated in middle school Field Hockey and Boys and Girls Cross Country and this winter we
   3|             will continue with middle school basketball after many years of it being eliminated. The opportunities middle school
   4|             sports provides to students to participate, learn and become part of an ettective team atmosphere is immeasurable.
   5|             Science Olympiad, Drama Club, Yearbook Club and middle school Student Government return as favorite activities
   6|              for students to engage in. Student Government continues to provide opportunities for students to engage in age
   7|              appropriate extracurricular activities like dances, spirit weeks and movie nights.
   8|             Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that ensure student
   9|             success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success continue to be our talented
  10|             faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and
  11|             learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to

  22|                                                                       LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
  23|             Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and opportunities for
  24|             students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and included in our community. LHS
  25|             faculty is student centered in their teaching practices, and strive to learn and to change in order to best serve all of our
  26|             2024 School Year
  27|             Lunenburg High School returned this August with a renewed philosophy of support for our school community. We have
  28|             assembled an outstanding, talented faculty with a shared vison of supporting students academically, socially and emo-
  29|             tionally as they tackle a rigorous and challenging curriculum. With our renewed sense of support, students are able to
  30|             acquire the skills necessary to earn a high school diploma as well as be successful in post-secondary education as well as
  31|             in the work force. One pillar of support that has proven to be successful for students is the LHS Intervention Teacher.
  32|             This teaching position provides direct instruction to students who were identified by their teachers as to needing addi-
  33|             tional support to be successful in the classroom. Students report to the Interventionist during their study skills block and
  34|             receive assistance to the specific class or classes they are struggling in. The data show the students participating in the
  35|             intervention classroom show substantial improvement in the completion of classwork and in their overall grades.
  36|                                                                                              111

===PAGE 112=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|             We continue to structure our Algebra classes to ensure all students are successful. We continue to keep class sizes low in
   2|             Algebra II that allow teachers to provide more personalized, individualized instruction while working with smaller
   3|             groups of students in a traditionally academically demanding course. This has been an effective strategy as you can see
   4|             though our results in the MCAS table below.
   5|             The restoration of faculty and courses at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options for course
   6|             selection, provided individualized and programmatic support and structure for students to be successful.
   7|                                                                                  MCAS RESULTS
   8|                                                Lunenburg High School - Spring 2024 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
   9|             Subject Test          Exceeding Expectations / Meeting Expectations                       State Average (EE/ME)
  10|             ELA                                          68%                                                     57%
  11|             Math                                         62%                                                     48%
  12|             Science                                      64%                                                     49%
  13|             The results indicate the LHS percentage scores for meeting and exceeding expectations are high, higher than the state
  14|             average and in mathematics, 14 percentage points higher than the state average when compared to schools who serve
  15|             students in similar grade levels. Reducing class sizes and providing students with additional individual classroom sup-
  16|             port was a successful strategy for improving student learning. The percentage of grade 10 students scoring in the meeting
  17|             and exceeding expectations category in mathematics is the highest percentage in the district.
  18|             We were also very pleased to learn that 30 students at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and Abigail Ad-
  19|             ams Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25
  20|             percent of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or
  21|             Advanced on both the English and Math exams. That is approximately 30% of grade 10 students receiving the award.

  21|             CURRICULUM
  22|             Teachers and administrators continue to refine, revise and create curriculum that is aligned to the state frameworks and is
  23|             rigorous and challenging. This year's focus is to work in vertical content area teams to ensure a smooth transition for
  24|             students as they advance grade by grade. This process also ensures that students will have and build upon the educational
  25|             skills that they will need to be successful in future grades. Scope and sequence maps have been created in order to assist
  26|             in this grade level curriculum mapping.
  27|             Lunenburg High School continues to provide a high quality education that ensures student success and promotes a pas-
  28|             sion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success continue to be our talented faculty, our parents and our
  29|             community members who support our teaching and learning. Most of all, our students who inspire us through their com-
  30|             mitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                     LETTER FROM LEADERSHIP
   2|                                                            In the long-storied history of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School
   3|                                                            District, I am confident that the 2023-2024 school year will stand out as one of the more
   4|                                                           memorable years in its workforce training legacy. Personally, it marked my second year
   5|                                                           as Superintendent and tenth, overall at Monty Tech. Thus, as a proud district leader, and
   6|                                                           in support of        the ground-breaking efforts of our students, faculty, staff                           and
   7|                                                           administration, it is my honor to highlight some of those initiatives with you, our school
   8|                                                           community, in this annual report.
   9|                                                            Throughout 2023-2024, Monty Tech took significant steps to address a growing concern
  10|                                                           facing vocational education in the Commonwealth - the overall lack of capacity to meet
  11|                                                           the demand for vocational training. In Monty Tech's case, well over 800 8th-grade
  12|                                                           students will typically apply for the approximately 350 available seats in the incoming
  13|                         freshmen class. Unable to simply accept more students here, Monty Tech implemented a creative strategy using a
  14|                          state-funded Lab Modernization grant to lease a nearby vacant warehouse with the intent of converting it into a
  15|                          contemporary workforce training facility that, when completed, will provide students training in House Carpentry,
  16|                          Electrical and Plumbing trades. The district navigated the design and permitting process that allowed for the initial
  17|                          renovation to begin, and formed meaningful partnerships with Fitchburg, Gardner and Narragansett School
  18|                          Districts.     The Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) approved the partnerships?
  19|                          applications, and as a result, the Montachusett Vocational Partnership (MVP) Academy was slated to begin in 2024-
  20|                          2025, serving as many as 84 high school students in its first year.
  21|                          While the MVP Program will result in the largest expansion of workforce training in our region, it represents only
  22|                          one dedicated effort to offer such instruction to a variety of constituencies. Monty Tech remains committed to
  23|                          offering career skills training to adult-students who are unemployed or underemployed by providing no-cost
  24|                          offerings in a number of fields. In the fall of 2023, Monty Tech was awarded a $720,000 Career Technical Initiative
  25|                          grant, so that even more adult learners would have an opportunity to earn valuable industry-recognized credentials,
  26|                          hands-on learning experiences, and receive job placement services upon program completion.
  27|                          Monty Tech also identified a previously underserved population, young adults with disabilities, and successfully
  28|                          delivered one of its most rewarding programs to date - the Supportive Training & Education Program (STEP) in
  29|                          the spring of 2024. Initiated through an award of $133,900 in funds made available through the Commonwealth
  30|                          Corporation, Monty Tech developed a 12-week program that included hands-on and professional skills training as
  31|                          well as two-weeks of paid internships through industry partners to twelve young adults (18-25 yrs. old) seeking an
  32|                         opportunity to overcome a variety of physical, emotional and cognitive disabilities, many of whom came from our
  33|                         nearby educational partner, CAPS Collaborative. In its pilot year, Monty Tech took great pride in knowing that we
  34|                          were the only regional vocational school district in the Commonwealth to participate in this meaningful workforce
  35|                          training initiative.
  36|                          A defining aspect of the Monty Tech experience is certainly our countless school-to-business partnerships,
  37|                          developed and maintained to benefit students and graduates alike. Last year, Monty Tech saw its largest number
  38|                          of students placed in real world learning opportunities through the school's Co-operative Education Program - 235
  39|                          seniors and second-semester juniors earned co-op positions, or more than 1 in 3 of all seniors and juniors, were
  40|                          placed by the end of the school year. These opportunities would not be possible without the ongoing support of our
  41|                          employer partners, and we remain so very grateful.
  42|                          We fully acknowledge that our success depends upon, and is wholly enriched by, the ongoing support of the civic
  43|                          leadership and citizens of our eighteen communities. On behalf of everyone at Monty Tech, thank you for your
  44|                          continued support. We are so honored to be an educational partner to all of our communities.
  45|                          Thomas R. Browne, Superintendent-Director
  46|                                                                                              117

   1|                                     OUR SCHOOL COMMUNITY
   2|                          Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School, more widely known as "Monty Tech," is a four-year
   3|                          career and       technical      high      school      serving eighteen member
   4|                          communities, including:

  21|                                                                        • Non-Binary          school currently offers only five non-traditional

   1|                                                       FINANCIAL REPORT
   2|                           FY 2022                                              FY 2023                                            FY 2024
   3|                            Educational Plan                                     Educational Plan                                   Educational Plan
   4|                              • $28,605,425                                      • $30,796,896                                        • $ 32,535,101

   6|                              • $3,734,533                                       • $2,963,607                                         • $2,760.905
   7|                          Each year, Monty Tech's Business Manager works in conjunction with the Superintendent and 22-member
   8|                          School Committee, to develop a cost-effective budget that reflects the values and priorities of the institution.
   9|                          The final fiscal year 2023-2024 Educational Plan totaled $32,535,101 which represented a 5.6% increase over
  10|                          the 2022-2023 Educational Plan, driven in large part by the 7.6% increase in the District's required minimum
  11|                          spending established by the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education. Remarkably, Monty Tech's
  12|                          FY24 budget only exceeded the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by
  13|                          $150,000 or 0.05%.
  14|                           While we are proud to offer vital education and training programs at a minimal cost to our member
  15|                          communities, we understand that vocational education is, in fact, more costly than a traditional, comprehensive
  16|                          academic program. To that end, school officials pursue countless grant opportunities to expand and improve
  17|                          training programs each year. For the fiscal year 2024, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
  18|                          $2,760,905. Programs funded by these grants include: Elementary and Secondary Schools Emergency Relief
  19|                          Funds, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support,
  20|                          Perkins Occupational Education, Vocational Capital Equipment Initiatives, Student Support and Academic
  21|                          Enrichment and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training.
  22|                          Expenses for the 2023-2024 school year included:
  23|                              District Expenses by Category

===PAGE 121=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                                                While students attending Monty Tech benefit from
   2|                                                                                                project-based, hands-on learning often associated with a
   3|                                                                                                vocational-technical high school experience, they must
   4|                                                                                                also complete all of the same academic requirements that
   5|                                                                                                their peers in comprehensive districts accomplish. At
   6|                                       ACADEMICS                                                Monty Tech, we believe a well-rounded high school
   7|                                                                                                experience includes rigorous and meaningful academic
   8|                          lessons. We are proud of our students' accomplishments in the classroom, as well as in their vocational shops.
   9|                          During the Spring of 2024, 10th grade students took the Next Generation MCAS in English Language Arts,
  10|                          Mathematics, and Biology. In addition, select 9th graders also sat for the Next Generation MCAS Biology
  11|                          exam. Students performed well across all subject areas, with a passing rate of 97% in English Language Arts,
  12|                          95% in Mathematics, and 97% in Biology. With regard to accountability, Monty Tech continues to make
  13|                          substantial progress toward targets.
  14|                          Monty Tech's academic programs continue to evolve to prepare graduates for diverse and challenging college
  15|                          and career pathways and are widely known to effectively prepare students at all levels to reach their goals. A
  16|                          focus in recent years has been the incorporation of new and updated materials and coursework to expand STEM
  17|                          opportunities for students with PLTW, AP, and dual enrollment options, especially in math and science. In
  18|                          addition, three of our science teachers are planning to attend the National Science Teachers Conference in
  19|                          2024 to explore innovative instructional practices and new technologies.
  20|                          In May of 2024, our students participated in AP exams and student performance showed outstanding results. In
  21|                          general, participation was strong with high numbers of students taking AP Exams (180 total). Those students
  22|                          took a record number of AP exams last year with a total of 262.
  23|                                                                                        2020              2021               2022               2023              2024
  24|                            Total AP Students                                            106               142                120               184                180
  25|                            Number of Exams                                              146               189                162               244                262
  26|                            AP Students with Scores 3+                                    75                61                 82               104                130
  27|                            % of Total AP Students with Scores 3+                     70.75%             42.96%            68.33%             56.52%             72.22%
  28|                          Since the pandemic, the exam process and format has shifted, but our results continue to show progress. Most
  29|                          importantly, a record number of Monty Tech students were eligible to receive college credit with qualifying
  30|                          scores of 3 or higher (130 total) - an impressive 72%. With regard to specific exams, 36 of our students took
  31|                          the exam for AP Seminar and AP Research. More than 90% of these Monty Tech students qualified for college
  32|                          credit. Not only did these students outperform all subjects, they performed better than most state, national,
  33|                          and global averages - an outstanding result for the second year of implementation. Finally, we had 19 AP
  34|                          Scholars - a growing number for Monty Tech (AP Scholars earn a qualifying score on at least 3 AP exams).
  35|                          Monty Tech continues to provide the following AP opportunities for students: AP English Literature &
  36|                          Composition, AP Language & Composition, AP Calculus, AP Statistics, AP Environmental Science, AP
  37|                          Chemistry, AP Physics, AP Psychology, AP Seminar, and AP Research. Due to the success of AP Seminar
  38|                          and AP Research, we plan to expand access to grade 10 students by adding a new course entitled English 10
  39|                                                                                              121

===PAGE 122=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                            VOCATIONAL TRAINING bE
   2|                          Preparing students for high-skill, high-wage careers requires technical proficiency, an ability to break down
   3|                          complex lessons into "bite sized" concepts, and a lot of patience. Our team of vocational instructors are not
   4|                          only skilled tradesmen and women, they are patient, collaborative and talented teachers, responsible for
   5|                          training the next generation's workforce. Their commitment to student achievement, community service, and
   6|                          advanced technical knowledge and skills is unmatched, and we are so grateful that they have chosen this
   7|                          profession, sharing their expertise with our students. 2023-2024 was a busy year across the District, with
   8|                          students working and learning in shops, on off-campus construction sites, and with area business leaders,
   9|                          refining their skills. We are proud to highlight some notable accomplishments.
  10|                          Advanced Manufacturing: Advanced Manufacturing students continue to work in a state-of-the-art machine
  11|                          shop and may earn a number of industry-recognized credentials to demonstrate their technical capacity. All
  12|                          junior students completed NC3 PMI 6 Certification. These students have 6 stackable credentials in precise
  13|                          measurement. The seniors earned an additional 6 stackable credentials in Advanced Measuring Instrument this
  14|                          year, which expands upon the PMI credentials earned in the Junior year, demonstrating more advanced
  15|                          measurement skills. All sophomores passed MACWIC level 1, while all Freshman students successfully
  16|                          completed safety training, earning an OSHA 10-hour general industry certification. The program is proud to
  17|                          have completed many projects for the community, while supporting fourteen Seniors and seven Juniors in their
  18|                          co-op placements, where they work and learn with employer partners in area machine shops. (Total student
  19|                          enrollment: 60)
  20|                                                                      Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: Monty Tech Auto Body students
  21|                                                                      earned valuable industry credentials in the 2023-2024 School year. Students
  22|                                                                      earned the OSHA 10-hour general industry card, EPA 6H spray certification,
  23|                                                                       ALI Lift Certification, I-CAR Pro-Level 1 in refinishing, and I-CAR Pro-
  24|                                                                       Level 1 in Collision Repair. Two Seniors and three juniors earned co-op
  25|                                                                       placements and were reported to be valuable contributors to local repair
  26|                                                                       shops in the area. At the SkillsUSA District Competition, students won a
  27|                                                                      gold and a bronze medal in collision repair, a silver and bronze in estimating,
  28|                                                                      and a gold medal in refinishing. A Junior student won a gold medal at the
  29|                                                                      SkillsUSA State competition and travelled to the National competition in
  30|                                                                      June 2024 to demonstrate their skills in Collision Repair. The program
  31|                                                                       worked to maintain the school's fleet of vehicles, vans, and trailers, and
  32|                                                                      painted the wiring rack for the MVP project and completed additional work
  33|                                                                      for Troop #193 Winchendon. (Total student enrollment: 65)
  34|                          Automotive Technology: Seniors in the Automotive Technology program earned their NC3/Snap-On Apollo
  35|                          D& Certification, while Juniors earned the NC3/Snap-On 525F Digital Multimeter certification. The Freshmen
  36|                          students earned the OSHA 10 Safety certification & the Shopkey Pro Service & Repair Information Level 1
  37|                          certification.. The sophomores received the Shopkey Pro & SureTrack Advanced Level 2 Certification. These
  38|                          industry-recognized credentials will enhance the student's level of competency within the automotive industry
  39|                          for years to come. Seven Seniors earned Co-op placements, while the shop continued to service, repair, and
  40|                          diagnose approximately four hundred vehicles throughout the year. These jobs included basic maintenance,
  41|                          inspections, alignments, brakes, exhaust, tires, and more advanced jobs including advanced electric diagnosis.
  42|                          Students worked closely with instructors to rewire, replace and add electrical devices to the trailer of Troop
  43|                                                                                              122

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                          #193 Winchendon, as well. An Automotive Technology Senior supported the program with his Eagle Badge
   2|                          Service Project, raising funds and purchasing six tool carts, six retractable air lines, as well as a numbering
   3|                          system and signage for our lifts. (Total student enrollment: 65)
   4|                          Business Technology: Monty Tech's Business Technology program continues to introduce students to a broad
   5|                          scope of financial literacy lessons. All Juniors and Seniors earned a financial literacy certificate from the
   6|                          MassMutual Foundation FutureSmart program. The 2023-2024 school year saw several top students earn
   7|                          recognition at the various SkillsUSA competitions, with one student competing in the Customer Service
   8|                          competition at the District level and a first-ever Entrepreneurship Team competing at the State level. Eight
   9|                          Seniors and three Juniors earned co-op placements, while all Freshmen and Sophomores completed the OSHA
  10|                          10-hour and CareerSafe Interview skills training and certification. (Total student enrollment: 65)
  11|                          Cabinetmaking: Monty Tech Cabinetmaking students are called upon for a number of projects, small and
  12|                          large, across the campus and beyond. During the 2023-2024 school year, students completed projects that
  13|                          included: installation of cabinets for the newly renovated Early Childhood classrooms; building and installing
  14|                          cabinets and new countertops in the Continuing Education Office; designing, constructing and installing three
  15|                          bulletin boards for the main corridors of Monty Tech; building a custom design, large birdcage for the school's
  16|                          Veterinary Science clinic; and building new cabinets and storage units for the district's Science classrooms.
  17|                          Ten Seniors and seven Juniors earned co-op placements, representing the program so well. Sophomores
  18|                          completed OSHA Construction 10-hour training and certification program, while Freshmen completed the
  19|                          OSHA Careersafe 10-hour online course. (Total student enrollment: 69)
  20|                          CAD/Drafting & Design: In 2023-2024, five Monty Tech CAD/Drafting & Design Senior students and one
  21|                          Junior earned co-op placements. Students were also able to achieve a number of valuable industry-recognized
  22|                          certifications including Autodesk Inventor, Revit, Autodesk AutoCAD and Solid Works. Freshmen students
  23|                          completed the 10-hour OSHA CareerSafe Online course. Two students competed in the SkillsUSA States
  24|                          Competition in Architectural Drafting and Technical Drafting. The shop completed a variety of signs, banners,
  25|                          and posters for the school, as well as layout designs for the Winchendon YMCA Playground and Monty Tech's
  26|                          proposed Auto Body/Collision Repair spray booth. (Total student enrollment: 55)
  27|                          Cosmetology: The Monty Tech Cosmetology program is one of the only programs that students may leave
  28|                          and directly enter the industry, fully licensed and ready for a career. The state-approved program is rigorous
  29|                          and provides students with countless opportunities to refine their cosmetology skills on clientele who
  30|                          understand and appreciate the learning environment. All 16 Seniors and five Juniors earned their cosmetology
  31|                          licenses — an incredibly successful pass rate and demonstration of the students' skills and abilities. Five Seniors
  32|                          participated in the job shadowing program, which gave them an opportunity to learn from practicing
  33|                                                                professionals, as well as offers for full-time employment prior to graduation!
  34|                                                                 (Total student enrollment: 84)
  35|                                                                 Culinary Arts: Monty Tech Culinary Arts students and instructors operate a full-
  36|                                                                 service restaurant and bakery, serving approximately 70-90 patrons daily. They
  37|                                                                 host a number of banquets and receptions throughout the year, to include:
  38|                                                                 Welcome Back/New Teachers reception, Principals & Counselors Day luncheon,
  39|                                                                 and two large scale Program Advisory Dinner/meetings annually. The program
  40|                                                                 continues to donate leftover baked goods to Our Father's House in Fitchburg and
  41|                                                                the Salvation Army in Fitchburg. Freshmen earned their OSHA 10-hour Culinary
  42|                                                                 certification, while Sophomores and Juniors earned their Allergen Awareness
  43|                                                                 certification, and Juniors and Seniors earned their Servsafe credentials. Juniors
  44|                                                                 took a field trip to Johnson and Wales University, and freshmen went to Gardner
  45|                                                                 Ale House, in Gardner, to learn about restaurant management. (Total student
  46|                                                                 enrollment: 68)
  47|                                                                                              123

===PAGE 124=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                          Dental Assisting: Monty Tech Dental Assisting instructors have emphasized the need for students to earn
   2|                          valuable industry-recognized credentials, as a means to demonstrate technical skill and proficiency. As a
   3|                          result, all students work and prepare for Dental Assisting National Board (DANB) certification exams. In
   4|                          2023-2024, all students passed the DANB Infection Control exam, and the DANB Radiology exam. This
   5|                          excellent pass rate is a true testament to the high-quality instruction in the Monty Tech Dental Assisting
   6|                          program, and something the school continues to be so proud of. A total of ten Seniors and three Juniors earned
   7|                          co-op placements, while the remaining Juniors and Seniors earned experience in affiliation/externship in area
   8|                          dental clinics and offices. All Seniors & Sophomores received the American Red Cross for Basic Life Support
   9|                          CPR/AED certification. Through the Community Health Connections Caring for Kids Program, Monty Tech
  10|                          students could receive free dental cleaning, radiographs, and sealants. Monty Tech Dental students were
  11|                          allowed to assist in their procedures. (Total student enrollment: 57)
  12|                          Early Childhood Education: Ten Seniors and one Junior in the school's Early Childhood Education program
  13|                          earned co-op placements, gaining valuable experience working with young children in local childcare centers.
  14|                          Three Seniors and one Juniors also gained experience on externships. All Sophomore and Seniors completed
  15|                          their First Aid/CPR training, and six Seniors also earned the Child Development Associate National
  16|                          certification. All thirteen Seniors worked with instructors to submit applications for the MA Department of
  17|                          Early Education and Care Teacher certification. Freshmen hosted a mock baby shower, collecting donations

  16|                                        COOPERATIVE EDUCATION
  17|                          The Monty Tech Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that
  18|                          combines classroom instruction with on-the-job-training. The popular Co-op Program provides students with
  19|                          an opportunity to further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry-related work
  20|                          environment. All students are eligible to participate in the Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and
  21|                          school grade, attendance and performance requirements, and all Co-op students must have completed the
  22|                           OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course prior to being placed in a work environment.
  23|                                               Students Participating in the Cooperative Education Program

===PAGE 128=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                          The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both students and employers. For the student, it is a
   2|                          chance to gain on-the-job training that improves both their technical proficiency and employability skills, and
   3|                          making industry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-op Program also
   4|                          allows students to earn while they learn, which has allowed many students to learn valuable life skills, financial
   5|                          literacy skills, and gain a deeper understanding of the importance of career advancement and self-advocacy.
   6|                          Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Co-op students provide a pool of
   7|                          trained talent, ready to assume temporary, part-time or even full-time opportunities, thus reducing employer training costs.
   8|                          Co-op work hours may also be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers.
   9|                           With approximately 100 area employers participating in Cooperative Education, providing valuable experience
  10|                          to 235 students by the end of the 2023-2024 school year, school officials remain grateful for their continued
  11|                          support, and look forward to future collaborations.
  12|                                STUDENT SUPPORT SERVICES
  13|                          Monty Tech's Department of Student Support Services houses the district's counseling services, oversees
  14|                          special education programming, and maintains efforts to provide social/emotional learning and resources to
  15|                          all students. Throughout the 2023-2024 school year, department staff continued to prioritize the district's effort
  16|                          to educate and develop the next generation of young adults, ensuring students have access to support in
  17|                          academic and vocational programming, as well as services related to social and emotional wellness.
  18|                                     and compassionate staff work to provide
  19|                          structured supports for students as they acclimated to
  20|                          the demands, pace and rigor of high school academics,
  21|                          vocational programming and extracurricular activities,
  22|                          understanding that a few short years ago, during the
  23|                          students' developmental years, students were isolated,
  24|                          had minimal opportunities to grow and develop
  25|                          through social interactions and hadn't experienced a                                        H235)
  26|                          'normal' school year. With increased exposure to
  27|                          technology and social media, the means in which
  28|                                                                       and connect
  29|                          shifted. With that, the foundational skills and social
  30|                          norms have also changed, taking on an array of
  31|                          variances that schools must recognize and address to
  32|                          effectively prepare students to be successful young
  33|                          adults.
  34|                          Recognizing the shift in experiences and brain development, Monty Tech has focused support on the growing
  35|                          concerns and struggles with age-appropriate interactions, use of social media, and the need for meaningful
  36|                          connections. The Department of Student Support has concentrated its efforts on creating a safe and supportive
  37|                          environment, offering opportunities and experiences that challenge our students to reflect, grow, and learn in
  38|                          all areas of their experiences. The department continued its important mission to break the stigma of mental

  41|                                                                                              128

   1|                          individually and meet success.
   2|                          In addition to the department's current school-based counseling services, Monty Tech and partner LUK, Inc.
   3|                          collaborated throughout 2023-2024 with the Department of Public Health to provide services to at-risk students
   4|                          and their families. The district also worked with LUK to create a direct pipeline for referrals and increased
   5|                          access to support. Programs such as the HOPEMatters, a peer-to-peer mental wellness and suicide prevention
   6|                          program, Project AMP, a program to address prevention of substance use among youth ages 13-17, and
   7|                          Students Taking A New Direction (STAND), a program to support students displaying at-risk behaviors, have
   8|                          all been implemented. These services allow students to have support beyond the school days and walls of
   9|                          Monty Tech.

  11|                          abuse counselors. CareSolace Coordinators address the growing need to locate support and match individuals
  12|                          with therapists, saving students and families hours of time, phone calls, waitlists and frustration. CareSolace
  13|                          supports the matching process and follows up with individuals to ensure each connection was a success. Most
  14|                          importantly, CareSolace Match Services are free to our students, family, staff and community:
  15|                          https://caresolace.com/site/montytech.

  17|                          specialized services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two hundred
  18|                          students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and approximately one hundred students adhering to
  19|                          individualized Section 504 plans. While the Student Support Services Department encompasses special
  20|                          education, the department provides support that is available to all Monty Tech students. Students have access
  21|                          to direct tutoring both before and after school. School counselors are available to assist students with
  22|                          vocational placement and post-secondary planning. Furthermore, our counselors are equipped with the skills
  23|                          to engage students in workshops and groups that support the development of social resilience, functional
  24|                          academics, finding balance, and referral services for those experiencing struggles.
